(42)

In the above, we pointed out that
\mathbf{V}
is invariant under both symmetries
\Xi
and
\PSL(2,\mathbb{Z})
only as a set, not pointwise. Also, the same is true about
\PSL(2,\mathbb{Z})
action on
\mathbf{D}_{1}
. While in the case of
\mathbf{V}
the reason for both claims is fairly clear (
e.g.
it is manifest in the
\tilde{t}\to 1
limit (
18
)), the fact that
\PSL(2,\mathbb{Z})
fixes
\mathbf{D}_{1}
only as a set and not pointwise is less obvious.In order to explain it, let us consider the limit
\tilde{t}=1+\epsilon
, with
\epsilon\ll 1
, and take
(x,y,z)=(2+a,2+b,2+c)
. Then, for small values of
(a,b,c)
, the surface (
14
) looks like a quadric

a^{2}+b^{2}+c^{2}-2(ab+bc+ca)=4\epsilon^{2}~,

on which the generators
\tau_{\pm}
act as linear reparametrizations:

\displaystyle\tau_{+}:(a,b,c)

\displaystyle\mapsto(a,2a+2b-c,b)~,

\displaystyle\tau_{-}:(a,b,c)

\displaystyle\mapsto(2a+2b-c,b,a)~.

2.2
DAHA of rank one and its spherical algebra

Now let us review a few necessary details of DAHA of rank one here.
Much like the Hecke algebra sits, loosely speaking, between the Weyl group and the braid group—in the sense that the latter two can be obtained by either specialization or by omitting some of the relations—DAHA sits in between the double affine Weyl group and the double affine braid group. This perspective, reviewed in e.g.
Guk 16
, will be useful to us in what follows. In Cartan type
A_{1}
, the
double affine braid group
(a.k.a. the
elliptic braid group
), denoted
\BB_{q=1}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})
, is simply the orbifold fundamental group of the quotient space
(T^{2}\backslash p)/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, the quotient of a once-punctured torus by
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. It is generated by three generators
X
,
Y
, and
T
, illustrated in Figure
3
:

\pi^{\textrm{orb}}_{1}\Bigl((T^{2}\backslash p)/\mathbb{Z}_{2}\Bigr)=\Bigl(T,X,Y|~TXT=X^{-1},\ \ TY^{-1}T=Y,\ \ Y^{-1}X^{-1}YXT^{2}=1\Bigr)~.

(43)

Its central extension, denoted
\BB(\mathbb{Z}_{2})
, is obtained by deforming the last relation to
Y^{-1}X^{-1}YXT^{2}=q^{-1}
.

T
Y
X

(a)

(b)

(c)

Figure 3:
Generators and relations in the orbifold fundamental group of the once-punctured torus. On the left, generators and relations are drawn on the double cover. The relations depicted are
TXT=X^{-1}
,
TY^{-1}T=Y
, and
Y^{-1}X^{-1}YXT^{2}=1
.

Then, rank-one DAHA
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is obtained by imposing one more quadratic (“Hecke”) relation:

\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})=\mathbb{C}_{q,t}\bigl[T^{\pm 1},X^{\pm 1},Y^{\pm 1}\bigr]\bigg/\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}TXT=X^{-1}~,&Y^{-1}X^{-1}YXT^{2}=q^{-1}~,\\
TY^{-1}T=Y~,&(T-t)(T+t^{-1})=0\end{array}\right\}~.

(44)

This involves the second deformation parameter
t
. Here
\mathbb{C}_{q,t}
is a ring of coefficients defined as follows.
Let
\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}},t^{\pm}]
be the ring of Laurent polynomials in the formal parameters
q^{1/2}
and
t
, and consider a multiplicative system
M
in
\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}},t^{\pm}]
generated by elements of the form
(q^{\ell}t-q^{-\ell}t^{-1})
for any non-negative integer
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. We define the coefficient ring
\mathbb{C}_{q,t}
to be the localization (or formal ‘‘fraction’’)
4
4

4

In other words,
\mathbb{C}_{q,t}
is the ring of rational functions in the formal parameters
q^{\frac{1}{2}}
and
t
where denominators are always elements in the multiplicative system
M
such as

\frac{f(X)}{(t-t^{-1})^{k_{0}}(qt-q^{-1}t^{-1})^{k_{1}}\cdots(q^{\ell}t-q^{-\ell}t^{-1})^{k_{\ell}}}~,\qquad f(X)\in\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}},t^{\pm},X^{\pm}]~.

of the ring
\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}},t^{\pm}]
at
M
:

\mathbb{C}_{q,t}=M^{-1}\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}},t^{\pm}]~.

(45)

This coefficient ring contains the two central generators of the algebra
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})
,
q
and
t
, which can be thought of as continuous deformation parameters and start life (in any irreducible representation) as arbitrary complex numbers. Many remarkable things happen when these two parameters assume special values, as will be further discussed in the sequel. In a way, the behavior of the algebra and its representations under such specializations—and the match of this behavior to the
A
-brane category—is one of the most interesting aspects of the geometric/physical approach.

Another standard notation for the second deformation parameter (which is convenient for some of the specializations) is

t=q^{c}~.

(46)

where
c
is often called the “central charge”. In what follows, we will use the shorthand notation
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}=\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})
unless we wish to make a statement about DAHA of Cartan type other than
A_{1}
.

For further details and properties of DAHA, we refer the reader to the fundamental book
Che 05
. The representation theory of DAHA there will be introduced throughout this section, as they emerge from physics and geometry.
Also, some basics of DAHA are assembled in Appendix
B
.

The construction of
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
based on the punctured torus allows us to see the action of the symmetry group (
31
), and the symmetry plays a pivotal role in the geometric understanding of the representation theory of (spherical) DAHA in what follows. Under
\Xi
, the generators are transformed as

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cccccc}\xi_{1}:&T\mapsto T,&X\mapsto-X,&Y\mapsto Y,&q\mapsto q,&t\mapsto t,\\
\xi_{2}:&T\mapsto T,&X\mapsto X,&Y\mapsto-Y,&q\mapsto q,&t\mapsto t~.\\
\end{array}

(47)

The mapping class group
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
acts on the generators of
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
as follows
5
5

5

Although we follow the notation of
Che 05
for the transformations
\tau_{\pm}
on the generators of DAHA here and in (
216
), we change matrix assignments to
\tau_{\pm}
as in (
38
) and (
218
) from
Che 05
since it is consistent with the projective action (
41
) of
\SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
on the exceptional divisors geometrically.
:

\displaystyle\tau_{+}

\displaystyle:\quad(X,Y,T)\mapsto(X,q^{-\frac{1}{2}}XY,T)

(48)

\displaystyle\tau_{-}

\displaystyle:\quad(X,Y,T)\mapsto(q^{\frac{1}{2}}YX,Y,T)

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle:\quad(X,Y,T)\mapsto(Y^{-1},XT^{2},T)

Since
\sigma
essentially exchanges the canonically conjugate variables
X
and
Y
, it is sometimes called the
Fourier transform
of
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
. Also,
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
enjoys the following (non-inner) involution,

\tilde{\iota}:T\mapsto-T,\quad X\mapsto X,\quad Y\mapsto Y,\quad q\mapsto q,\quad t\mapsto t^{-1}~.\\

(49)

It is easy to check from the Hecke relation that
\mathbf{e}=({T+t^{-1}})/({t+t^{-1}})
is an idempotent element (
\mathbf{e}^{2}=\mathbf{e}
) of
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
.
Then, the spherical subalgebra
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
is defined by the idempotent projection

S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}:=\mathbf{e}\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}\mathbf{e}~.

(50)

The generators of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
can be identified with

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=(1+t^{2})\mathbf{e}X\mathbf{e}=(X+X^{-1})\mathbf{e}

(51)

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=(1+t^{-2})\mathbf{e}Y\mathbf{e}=(Y+Y^{-1})\mathbf{e}

(52)

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=(q^{-\frac{1}{2}}Y^{-1}X+q^{\frac{1}{2}}X^{-1}Y)\mathbf{e}=\frac{[x,y]_{q}}{(q^{-1}-q)}~,

(53)

and they satisfy relations

\displaystyle[x,y]_{q}

\displaystyle=(q^{-1}-q)z

(54)

\displaystyle{}[y,z]_{q}

\displaystyle=(q^{-1}-q)x

\displaystyle{}[z,x]_{q}

\displaystyle=(q^{-1}-q)y

\displaystyle q^{-1}x^{2}+qy^{2}+q^{-1}z^{2}-q^{-\frac{1}{2}}xyz

\displaystyle=(q^{-\frac{1}{2}}t-q^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{-1})^{2}+(q^{\frac{1}{2}}+q^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{2}~,

where
q=e^{2\pi i\hbar}
and the
q
-commutator is defined by

[a,b]_{q}:=q^{-\frac{1}{2}}ab-q^{\frac{1}{2}}ba~.

See
e.g.

Ter 13
for further details. The key point is that the spherical DAHA
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
is commutative at the “classical” limit
q=1
while the DAHA
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
is not commutative even in the
q=1
limit. Indeed, it is easy to see that in the “classical” limit
q\to 1
, the Casimir relation (the last one) in (
54
) becomes the equation for the cubic surface (
14
):

S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}\xrightarrow[q\to 1]{}\mathscr{O}(\mathcal{M}_{\text{flat}}(C_{p},\SL(2,\mathbb{C})))~.

(55)

Thus,
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
is the deformation quantization
\mathscr{O}^{q}(\mathfrak{X})
of the coordinate ring (
14
) of the moduli space of flat
\SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-connections
\mathfrak{X}=\mathcal{M}_{\text{flat}}(C_{p},\SL(2,\mathbb{C}))
with respect to the Poisson bracket defined by
\Omega_{J}

Obl04a
;
Obl04b
.

Here, it is worth commenting on an important issue in the context of the deformation quantization of the coordinate ring on the affine cubic hypersurface of the form (
14
). It is clear that this equation is Weyl-group invariant, so that the monodromy parameter
\tilde{t}
appears only through the symmetric combination
\tilde{t}+\tilde{t}^{-1}
, and that the same symmetry applies to the Poisson structure. Moreover, if we work with a specific value of
\tilde{t}
, we will obtain the deformation quantization at a specific value of the parameters,
i.e.
for a specific choice of the central character (at least for the formal parameter
t
).

Since the inputs to deformation quantization depend on
\tilde{t}
only in a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-invariant fashion, the output
\mathscr{O}^{q}(\mathfrak{X}_{\tilde{t}})
will also have the corresponding symmetry. However, this clarifies that
\tilde{t}\neq t
, since the relations (
54
) do
not
depend symmetrically on
t
. The proper identification is

\tilde{t}=tq^{-\frac{1}{2}}~,

(56)

as will be made clear by the discussion of the formal outer automorphism
\iota
below. There is no contradiction with the statement that
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
is the deformation quantization of
\mathscr{O}(\mathfrak{X})
, since the classical limit of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
still recovers the same commutative Poisson algebra.

It is simple to check that the two involutions (
47
) straightforwardly reduce to the symmetry of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
, which is the same as (
33
). As in the classical case, the non-trivial central element
-1\in\SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
acts trivially on the generators of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
, and the action of
\PSL(2,\mathbb{Z})
is quantized from (
40
)

\displaystyle\tau_{+}

\displaystyle:\quad(x,y,z)\mapsto\Bigl(x,\frac{xy+yx}{q^{1/2}+q^{-1/2}}-z,y\Bigr)~,

(57)

\displaystyle\tau_{-}

\displaystyle:\quad(x,y,z)\mapsto\Bigl(\frac{xy+yx}{q^{1/2}+q^{-1/2}}-z,y,x\Bigr)~,

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle:\quad(x,y,z)\mapsto\Bigl(y,x,\frac{xy+yx}{q^{1/2}+q^{-1/2}}-z\Bigr)~.

Thus, the symmetries
\Xi\times\PSL(2,\mathbb{Z})
can be seen in outer automorphisms of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
.
The other outer automorphism

\tilde{\iota}
in (
49
) is somewhat more complicated; it does not preserve the idempotent, but it rather brings it into the other idempotent element

\tilde{\iota}:\quad\mathbf{e}=\frac{T+t^{-1}}{t+t^{-1}}\mapsto\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}=\frac{-T+t}{t+t^{-1}}~.

(58)

Thus,
\tilde{\iota}
maps
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
to the other spherical subalgebra
\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}
where the Casimir relations are different by
t\leftrightarrow t^{-1}
. However, the involution
\tilde{\iota}
on
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
does correspond in a sense to an outer automorphism of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
, which acts simply by

\iota:t\mapsto qt^{-1}.

(59)

Indeed, it is easy to check that this map preserves the Casimir relation in (
54
). (Note that this automorphism only acts nontrivially when
q
and
t
are regarded as formal elements; it does not preserve the central character.)

In general, we are free to think of any commutative algebra as the coordinate ring of a certain affine space. In addition to the example above, we consider the case of
\mathfrak{X}=\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}
for the quantum torus algebra in Appendix
C
, and
\mathfrak{X}
as 3d
\mathcal{N}=4
Coulomb branches in Appendix
D
in this paper. What is common between all of these examples are certain key properties of
\mathfrak{X}
: First of all, it will always be a non-compact manifold, so that it has a large and interesting algebra
\mathscr{O}(\mathfrak{X})
of holomorphic functions with polynomial growth at infinity. (In fact, in this paper,
\mathfrak{X}
will always be an affine variety over
\mathbb{C}
.)
It will also be a hyper-Kähler manifold, and an algebra is obtained by the deformation quantization of the coordinate ring of
\mathfrak{X}
with respect to a certain holomorphic symplectic form.
These conditions fit into the context of brane quantization
GW 09
in a 2d sigma-model. It is the central idea of this paper, and this will pave the way towards a geometric angle on the representation theory of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
.

2.3
Canonical coisotropic branes in
A
-models

Here, we will obtain the deformation quantization of the coordinate ring of
\mathfrak{X}
with respect to
\Omega_{J}
by using the 2d
A
-model on a symplectic manifold
(\mathfrak{X},\omega_{\mathfrak{X}})
. The main character in our story is the
canonical coisotropic brane
, denoted
\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}}
. Eventually, we will investigate the representation theory of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
by the 2d
A
-model, but we begin by constructing the (presumably less familiar) canonical coisotropic brane
\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}}
here. Subsequently, we will discuss standard Lagrangian branes and some methods for computing spaces of morphisms in what follows. Our review is necessarily cursory; for more details, we refer to the literature
GW 09
;
Guk 11
.

In general, as was pointed out in
KO 03
, the
A
-model admits branes with support on coisotropic submanifolds which are equipped with a transverse holomorphic structure. The canonical coisotropic brane is supported on the target space
\mathfrak{X}
itself, which is a coisotropic submanifold of the target space
\mathfrak{X}
in a rather trivial way. More precisely, there is a family of such branes, labeled by a complex parameter

\hbar=|\hbar|e^{i\theta}~,

(60)

and we will identify it with the parameter of deformation quantizations by
q=e^{2\pi i\hbar}
. The fact that the support involves no additional choice is (at least part of) the reason for the term “canonical.” On a
2n
-dimensional target space, coisotropic branes can therefore be supported in dimension
n+2j
for integer
j
; when
n
is even, there can be branes supported throughout the entire target. In our example,
n=2
, so that no other coisotropic branes can occur just for dimension reasons.

In complex structure
\mathcal{I}=I\cos\theta-K\sin\theta
, the data defining the brane
\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}}
is simply a holomorphic line bundle
\mathcal{L}\to\mathfrak{X}
, equipped with a connection whose curvature
F
is of course equal to the first Chern class:

\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}}:\quad\hbox to26.11pt{\vbox to46.31pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 13.05554pt\lower-23.15279pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-13.05554pt}{-19.49306pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{
{
\lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}
{}{}{}
\lxSVG@stroke}}
}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 12.18 L 0 -11.62}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.0}{-1.0}{1.0}{0.0}{0.0pt}{-8.60004pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\qquad\qquad c_{1}(\mathcal{L})=[F/2\pi]\in H^{2}(\mathfrak{X},\mathbb{Z})~.

(61)

As usual, open strings ending on
\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}}
source the gauge-invariant combination
F+B
, where

B\in H^{2}(\mathfrak{X},\U(1))

(62)

is the 2-form
B
-field.
For our family of the canonical coisotropic branes
\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}}
parametrized by
\hbar
on a symplectic manifold
(\mathfrak{X},\omega_{\mathfrak{X}})
, the values of
[B/2\pi]\in H^{2}(\mathfrak{X},\U(1))
and the integral class
[F/2\pi]\in H^{2}(\mathfrak{X},\mathbb{Z})
are determined by the equation

\Omega:=F+B+i\omega_{\mathfrak{X}}=\frac{\Omega_{J}}{i\hbar}~,

(63)

so that at a generic value of
\hbar
in (
60
) we can write

\displaystyle F+B

\displaystyle=\Real\,\Omega=\frac{1}{|\hbar|}(\omega_{I}\cos\theta-\omega_{K}\sin\theta)~,

(64)

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathfrak{X}}

\displaystyle=\Imaginary\,\Omega=-\frac{1}{|\hbar|}(\omega_{I}\sin\theta+\omega_{K}\cos\theta)~.

(65)

Since the hyper-Kähler conditions ensure that
J=\omega_{\mathfrak{X}}^{-1}(F+B)
, we have the condition for
\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}}
to be a coisotropic
A
-brane
KO 03

\big(\omega_{\mathfrak{X}}^{-1}(B+F)\big)^{2}=J^{2}=-1~.

(66)

In particular, when
\hbar
is real,
\omega_{\mathfrak{X}}=\omega_{K}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}}
is a brane of type
(B,A,A)
, whereas for
\hbar
purely imaginary,
\omega_{\mathfrak{X}}=\omega_{I}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}}
is an
(A,A,B)
-brane.
\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}}
is also called “canonical” because its extra data corresponds in this fashion to the holomorphic symplectic structure.

Now comes the key point.
Under this circumstance, the space
\Hom(\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}},\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}})
of open
(\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}},\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}})
strings with both ends
on the canonical coisotropic brane
\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}}
is a non-commutative deformation of the Dolbeault cohomology
H_{\overline{\partial}}^{0,*}(\mathfrak{X})
when
\mathfrak{X}
is regarded as a complex manifold with
J
, and we are interested in its zeroth degree, namely the space of holomorphic functions.
Moreover, for
\mathfrak{X}
an affine variety, a suitable condition at infinity for a “good
A
-model” is to allow only functions of polynomial growth.
In the presence of non-trivial background
F+B\neq 0
,
\Hom^{0}(\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}},\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}})
is therefore the deformation quantization of the coordinate ring on
\mathfrak{X}
,
holomorphic in complex structure
J

AZ 05
;
GW 09
.
6
6

6

Since we are mainly interested in the zeroth degree of morphism spaces, we will usually omit the superscript
0
, meaning
\Hom=\Hom^{0}
unless it is specified.

In general, for any brane
\mathfrak{B}
, in either the
A
-model or the
B
-model, the space of open strings states
\End(\mathfrak{B})
forms an algebra. This can be easily understood by considering the process of joining open strings, illustrated in Figure
4
(left).
However, generically, this algebra of
(\mathfrak{B},\mathfrak{B})
strings is rather simple and not very interesting. Even if the brane
\mathfrak{B}
is “big enough,” the algebra
\End(\mathfrak{B})
can be interesting, but may be hard to identify or relate to more familiar algebras. For example, various
(B,B,B)
branes represented by hyper-holomorphic sheaves in
Guk 11
lead to interesting endomorphism algebras, but apart from some special cases it is hard to recognize them in the world of more familiar algebras.
What makes the canonical coisotropic brane special is that the algebra
\End(\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}})
can be identified with the deformation quantization
\mathscr{O}^{q}(\mathfrak{X})
of the target manifold
\mathfrak{X}

KW 07
.

Figure 4:
(Left) Open strings that start and end on the same brane
\mathfrak{B}
form an algebra.

(Right) Joining a
(\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}},\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}})
-string with a
(\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}},\mathfrak{B}^{\prime})
-string leads to another
(\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}},\mathfrak{B}^{\prime})
-string.

2.3.1
Spherical DAHA as the algebra of
(\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}},\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}})
-strings

In our example, the target space
\mathfrak{X}=\mathcal{M}_{\text{flat}}(C_{p},\SL(2,\mathbb{C}))
is the moduli space of flat
\SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-connections over a punctured torus, which is a hyper-Kähler manifold. Then, by construction, the algebra of
(\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}},\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}})
strings is the deformation quantization
\mathscr{O}^{q}(\mathfrak{X})
of the coordinate ring on
\mathfrak{X}
with respect to
\Omega_{J}
, which is the spherical DAHA
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
.

The parameter
q
of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
is identified with
\hbar
in the data (
63
) of
\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}}
via
q=\exp(2\pi i\hbar)
.
It is worth emphasizing that for a generic value of
q\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, the
B
-field needs to be turned on in the sigma-model. In fact, the target admits the Hitchin fibration (
19
) where a generic fiber is a two-torus
T^{2}
. Since a generic fiber
\mathbf{F}
is Lagrangian with respect to
\omega_{J}
and
\omega_{K}
and it sees only
\omega_{I}
, the evaluation of
\Omega
in (
63
) over
\mathbf{F}
yields

\int_{\mathbf{F}}\frac{\Omega}{2\pi}=\frac{1}{\hbar}~,

where
F+B
is responsible for its real part.
Because
[F/2\pi]\in H^{2}(\mathfrak{X},\mathbb{Z})
is an element of the second integral cohomology class, the
B
-field needs to be switched on unless the real value of
1/\hbar
is an integer. Thus, a 2d
A
-model has to incorporate the
B
-field for a generic value of
\hbar
, and we will moreover witness that the
B
-field plays a more important role in the subsequent sections.

The parameter
t
of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
is related to the ramification parameters of the target space. In fact, the monodromy parameter (
15
) around the puncture can be expressed by the ramification parameters (
11
) as

\tilde{t}=\exp(-\pi(\tgamma_p+i \talpha_p))~.

Furthermore, (
56
) compares the monodromy parameter
\tilde{t}
with the central character
t
of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
. Then, it is easy to see from (
28
) that the evaluation of (
63
) on an exceptional divisor yields

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbf{D}_{i}}F+B+i\omega_{\mathfrak{X}}=\int_{\mathbf{D}_{i}}\frac{\Omega_{J}}{2\pi i\hbar}=\frac{{}_{p}+i\talpha_{p}}{2i\hbar}=-c+\frac{1}{2}~.

(67)

where
c
is the central charge in (
46
).

The canonical coisotropic brane enjoys the symmetries
\Xi\times\PSL(2,\mathbb{Z})
of the target space
\mathfrak{X}
analyzed in §
2.1
, which become the outer automorphisms of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
given by (
33
) and (
57
). The symmetry (
59
) of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
is indeed the Weyl group symmetry
\tilde{t}\leftrightarrow\tilde{t}^{-1}
of the monodromy matrix (
15
). In fact, the Weyl group symmetry (
29
) of the ramification parameters preserves the target space. Since the canonical coisotropic brane is sensitive only to
(\talpha_{p},{}_{p})
or
\tilde{t}
, the symmetry (
59
) of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
is equivalent to the fact that the canonical coisotropic branes supported on
\mathfrak{X}_{\tilde{t}}
and
\mathfrak{X}_{\tilde{t}^{-1}}
related by the Weyl group symmetry give rise to the isomorphic algebra

\End(\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}})\cong S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}\cong\End(\iota(\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}}))~.

(68)

2.4
Lagrangian
A
-branes and modules of
\mathscr{O}^{q}(\mathfrak{X})

Now we lay out the approach to the representation theory of
\mathscr{O}^{q}(\mathfrak{X})
by the 2d
A
-model on
(\mathfrak{X},\omega_{\mathfrak{X}})
.
This subsection also serves as a lightning review about the category of
A
-branes.

The approach to the representation theory of
\mathscr{O}^{q}
from the 2d
A
-model arises from a simple idea: given an open string boundary condition (or an
A
-brane)
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
, the space of
(\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}},\mathfrak{B}^{\prime})
open strings forms a vector space. As in the right of Figure
4
, a joining of
(\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}},\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}})
and
(\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}},\mathfrak{B}^{\prime})
string leads to another
(\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}},\mathfrak{B}^{\prime})
string. This implies that the space of
(\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}},\mathfrak{B}^{\prime})
strings receives an action of the algebra of
(\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}},\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}})
strings
GW 09
. Namely, other
A
-branes
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
on
\mathfrak{X}
give rise to modules for
\mathscr{O}^{q}(\mathfrak{X})
:

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathscr{O}^{q}(\mathfrak{X})&=&\Hom(\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}},\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}})\\
\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\circlearrowright$}&&\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\circlearrowright$}\\
\mathscr{B}^{\prime}&=&\Hom(\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}},\mathfrak{B}^{\prime})\end{array}

(69)

In our example, supports of other branes
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
are always Lagrangian submanifolds so that we will review Lagrangian
A
-branes
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathbf{L}}
in the next subsection.
If the support of
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
is a Lagrangian submanifold contained in the fixed point set of an antiholomorphic involution
\zeta:\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{X}
with
\zeta^{*}J=-J
, then the corresponding representation admits unitarity.

We now briefly recall a few standard facts about Lagrangian
A
-branes
Flo 88
;
Flo 89
and their mathematical incarnation, the Fukaya category
\Fuk(\mathfrak{X},\omega_{\mathfrak{X}})
. For more detail, the reader is referred to the literature, which is substantial;
Aur 14
is a good starting point, or
Kon 95
for the fundamentals of homological mirror symmetry.

The
Lagrangian Grassmannian
, denoted
\LagGr
, of a symplectic vector space parameterizes the collection of its Lagrangian subspaces. We can obtain a description of this space by thinking of the standard symplectic vector space
(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\omega)
, which can be equipped with a metric via a contractible choice. By the two-of-three property, the group preserving both the symplectic and orthogonal structures is
\U(n)
, which therefore acts on
\LagGr(2n)
; the subgroup stabilizing a fixed Lagrangian subspace is
\Or(n)
, so that

\LagGr(2n)=\U(n)/\Or(n)~.

(70)

There is furthermore an obvious map

{\det}^{2}:\LagGr(2n)\to\U(1)

(71)

which can be shown to induce an isomorphism of fundamental groups. The
Maslov index

Arn 67
of a loop in
\LagGr(2n)
is its image under this induced map in
\pi_{1}(\U(1))\cong\mathbb{Z}
; it is responsible for both obstructions and gradings in the Fukaya category. The universal cover
\widetilde{\LagGr}(2n)
of
\LagGr(2n)
thus has deck group
\mathbb{Z}
, and the Maslov index of a loop is simply the element of
\mathbb{Z}
that connects the endpoints of a lift to
\widetilde{\LagGr}(2n)
.

Let
(\mathfrak{X},\omega_{\mathfrak{X}})
be a symplectic manifold with zero first Chern class (as is obviously the case in our hyper-Kähler examples). There is a bundle

\LagGr(\mathfrak{X})\to\mathfrak{X}

(72)

whose fiber over
x\in\mathfrak{X}
is
\LagGr(T_{x}\mathfrak{X})
. (We hope the reader will forgive the moderately abusive notation.)
We can furthermore define a bundle
\widetilde{\LagGr}(\mathfrak{X})
, which is a covering space of the total space
\LagGr(\mathfrak{X})
, such that the covering map is a bundle map and restricts over each fiber to the universal covering map.

A Lagrangian subspace
\mathbf{L}\subset\mathfrak{X}
comes with an obvious lift

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\LagGr(\mathfrak{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}

(73)

defined by the Lagrangian subbundle
T\mathbf{L}\subset\left.T\mathfrak{X}\right|_{\mathbf{L}}
. Lifting this canonical map to
\widetilde{\LagGr}(\mathfrak{X})
is obstructed by the image of
\pi_{1}(\mathbf{L})
under the Maslov map, which is an element of
H^{1}(\mathbf{L},\mathbb{Z})
called the
Maslov class
. Lagrangians with zero Maslov class admit so-called
graded lifts
, which are maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\LagGr}(\mathfrak{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\LagGr(\mathfrak{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot/\mathbb{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}

(74)

making the square commute. The set of such maps is naturally a
\mathbb{Z}
-torsor under the action of deck transformations, but no canonical choice of graded lift exists. Given a Lagrangian object of
\text{$A$-}{\Brane}(\mathfrak{X},\omega_{\mathfrak{X}})
, the set of graded lifts plays the role of its shifts.

A (rank-one) Lagrangian object of
\text{$A$-}{\Brane}(\mathfrak{X},\omega_{\mathfrak{X}})
is supported on a Lagrangian submanifold
\mathbf{L}\subset\mathfrak{X}
of zero Maslov class, which is considered up to Hamiltonian isotopy. The additional data required to define a Lagrangian
A
-brane consists of a “Chan-Paton” bundle with unitary connection; a flat
\Spin^{c}
structure on
\mathbf{L}
; and a grade lift.
A Chan-Paton bundle for a Lagrangian
A
-brane is generally endowed with a flat
\Spin^{c}
structure
Wit 98
;
FW 99
;
KS 03
;
GW 09
.
A
\Spin^{c}
structure arises if
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
does not exist as a line bundle, but is obstructed
by the same cocycle that obstructs the existence of the square root
K_{\mathbf{L}}^{-1/2}
of the canonical bundle over the Lagrangian
\mathbf{L}
. Namely, putative transition functions
g_{ij}
and
w_{ij}
of
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
and
K_{\mathbf{L}}^{-1/2}
, respectively, obey
g_{ij}g_{jk}g_{ki}=\phi_{ijk}=w_{ij}w_{jk}w_{ki}
where
\phi_{ijk}=\pm 1
. In this case, the cocycle cancels out in the transition functions
g_{ij}w_{ij}
of an honest vector bundle
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\otimes K_{\mathbf{L}}^{-1/2}\to\mathbf{L}
, called a
\Spin^{c}
structure. The
K_{\mathbf{L}}^{-1/2}
part in a
\Spin^{c}
structure arises from boundary fermions of the open worldsheet
(
Hor 00
, §5)

(
HHP 08
, §3.2)
, which gives rise to a
\Spin^{c}
structure of the normal bundle to the support of a brane. (This proposal is explicitly checked by Hemisphere partition functions in
KLY 14
.) Thus, the canonical coisotropic brane
\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}}
is endowed with an ordinary line bundle whereas a Lagrangian
A
-brane is equipped with a
\Spin^{c}
structure.
Since most of the Lagrangian submanifolds in this paper are of real two dimensions, there always exists a spin bundle of
\mathbf{L}
, which is a square-root of the canonical bundle
K_{\mathbf{L}}^{\pm 1/2}
of
\mathbf{L}
, though it is not necessarily unique. Hence, both
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
and
K_{\mathbf{L}}^{-1/2}
exist as genuine line bundles in most of the examples in this paper and we treat their tensor product
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\otimes K_{\mathbf{L}}^{-1/2}
as a
\Spin^{c}
structure. However, a subtlety arises when an
A
-brane degenerates into a different spin structure, which will be considered in §
2.7
. Moreover, a Lagrangian
A
-brane is endowed with a flat
\Spin^{c}
structure:
if
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
exists as a line bundle, the curvature
F^{\prime}_{\mathbf{L}}
of
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
must obey a gauge-invariant version of the flatness condition

F^{\prime}_{\mathbf{L}}+\left.B\right|_{\mathbf{L}}=0~,

(75)

in the presence of a
B
-field. Even if
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
does not exist as a line bundle, its square
(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})^{2}
does so that a half of the curvature of
(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})^{2}
is subject to (
75
).
In sum, for a Lagrangian
A
-brane, we have a Chan-Paton bundle

\mathfrak{B}_{\mathbf{L}}:\quad\hbox to62.03pt{\vbox to49.88pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 31.01389pt\lower-24.93948pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-31.01389pt}{-21.27975pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 9.74 L 0 -14.06}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.0}{-1.0}{1.0}{0.0}{0.0pt}{-10.35893pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(76)

with a flat
\Spin^{c}
structure (
75
).
We will sometimes denote a Chan-Paton bundle by
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathbf{L}}\to\mathbf{L}
, abusing notation.
Morphisms between Lagrangian objects are defined in the usual way using the Floer–Fukaya complex generated by intersection points between the Lagrangians; see
Aur 14
for details.

Defining the space of morphisms between Lagrangian and coisotropic objects is a bit more subtle, and is discussed in detail for flat targets in
AZ 05
. The essential idea is that the morphism space should be thought of as related to the space of holomorphic functions on the intersection, with respect to the transverse holomorphic structure on coisotropic objects. For Lagrangian objects, this complex structure obviously plays no role, but instanton corrections can appear, in the guise of the contributions of holomorphic disks to the differential in the Floer–Fukaya complex. On the other hand, for
\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}}
, the transverse holomorphic structure is just a standard complex structure and plays an essential role, but instanton corrections are forbidden. In the case of general coisotropic branes, both phenomena can be expected to be relevant. (For some further discussion of this fact from the worldsheet perspective, as well as generalizations to branes of higher rank, see
Her 12
.)

In a hyper-Kähler manifold, we can make use of a
B
-model analysis as in
GW 09
;
Guk 11
to compute the dimension of open strings. The dimension of the representation space
\mathscr{L}:=\Hom(\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}},\mathfrak{B}_{\mathbf{L}})
associated to a compact Lagrangian brane
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathbf{L}}
can be computed with the help of the Grothendieck–Riemann–Roch formula:

\displaystyle\dim\mathscr{L}

\displaystyle=\dim H^{0}(\mathbf{L},\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}}\otimes\mathfrak{B}_{\mathbf{L}}^{-1})

(77)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{L}}\text{ch}(\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}})\wedge\text{ch}(\mathfrak{B}_{\mathbf{L}}^{-1})\wedge\Td(T\mathbf{L})~,

Here we denote, by
\mathfrak{B}
, a bundle for the corresponding brane including an effect of the
B
-field, abusing notation.

If a Lagrangian
\mathbf{L}
is of real two dimensions, then the Todd class
\Td(T\mathbf{L})=\text{ch}(K_{\mathbf{L}}^{-1/2})\widehat{A}(T\mathbf{L})
is equivalent to
\text{ch}(K_{\mathbf{L}}^{-1/2})
. Consequently, the formula becomes a very simple form

\dim\mathscr{L}=\int_{\mathbf{L}}\text{ch}(\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}})=\int_{\mathbf{L}}\frac{F+B}{2\pi}~,

(78)

for a real two-dimensional Lagrangian
\mathbf{L}
.

As explained in
GW 09
, for a Lagrangian brane
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathbf{L}}
, the space of open strings
\Hom(\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}},\mathfrak{B}_{\mathbf{L}})
can be understood as a geometric quantization of
\mathbf{L}
with a curvature on a “prequantum line bundle”
\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}}\otimes\mathfrak{B}_{\mathbf{L}}^{-1}
. If
\mathfrak{X}
is a complexification of
\mathbf{L}
in the sense of
GW 09
, then the action of
\End(\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}})
on the quantization
\Hom(\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}},\mathfrak{B}_{\mathbf{L}})
plays the role of the quantized algebra of operators.

Finally, let us mention a brief word about coefficients. In general, the Fukaya category is defined with coefficients in the Novikov ring; this is necessary because the sums over instanton contributions that define the differential are formal and not necessarily guaranteed to converge. Similarly, deformation quantization of a Poisson manifold
Gro 46
;
Fed 94
;
Kon 03
is not guaranteed to produce convergent series, but only a formal deformation in general. We will restrict ourselves to target spaces
\mathfrak{X}
for which a “good
A
-model” is expected to exist, meaning that all the series involved should in fact converge. The existence of a complete hyper-Kähler metric on
\mathfrak{X}
should be sufficient to ensure this; see
GW 09
for further discussion of this issue.

We will proceed to compare the two categories
\text{$A$-}{\Brane}(\mathfrak{X},\omega_{\mathfrak{X}})
and
\Rep(S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H})
via the brane quantization.
7
7

7

Note, that spherical DAHA is Morita-equivalent to DAHA (
44
),
i.e
the category of representations of DAHA is equivalent to the category of representations of its spherical subalgebra
Obl04b
:

\Rep(\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H})\cong\Rep(S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H})~.

(79)

See also (
208
) for the explanation from the 2d sigma-model.

For the comparison, the symmetries play a crucial role. In fact, the symmetries of the target space
\mathfrak{X}
become the group of auto-equivalences of the categories. More concretely, we will investigate the action of
\Xi\times\PSL(2,\mathbb{Z})
((
33
) and (
57
)) and the Weyl group
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
generated by
\iota
(
59
) on both categories.

Now we set up the framework so that we will start our expedition to “see” and “touch” representations of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
as if they were geometric objects in the target
\mathfrak{X}
.

2.5
(A,B,A)
-branes for polynomial representations

DAHA was introduced by Cherednik in the study of Macdonald polynomials from the viewpoint of representation theory
Che95a
in which the distinguished infinite-dimensional representation on the ring
\mathscr{P}:=\mathbb{C}_{q,t}[X^{\pm}]^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
of symmetric Laurent polynomials, called
polynomial representation
, plays an important role. (See also
Che 17
for finite-dimensional modules.) Here, Laurent polynomials in a single variable
X
over
\mathbb{C}_{q,t}
are symmetrized under the inversion
\mathbb{Z}_{2}:X\mapsto X^{-1}
so that the ring can also be expressed as
\mathscr{P}=\mathbb{C}_{q,t}[X+X^{-1}]
.
This polynomial representation of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
is defined by the following formulas:

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto X+X^{-1},

(80)

\displaystyle\pol:S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}\to\End(\mathscr{P}),\quad y

\displaystyle\mapsto\frac{tX-t^{-1}X^{-1}}{X-X^{-1}}\varpi+\frac{t^{-1}X-tX^{-1}}{X-X^{-1}}\varpi^{-1},

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle\mapsto q^{\frac{1}{2}}X\frac{tX-t^{-1}X^{-1}}{X-X^{-1}}\varpi+q^{\frac{1}{2}}X^{-1}\frac{t^{-1}X-tX^{-1}}{X-X^{-1}}\varpi^{-1},

where
\varpi^{\pm}(X)=q^{\pm}X
is the exponentiated degree operator, often called the
q
-shift operator, that appeared in (
266
) for the quantum torus algebra. In particular,
\pol(y)
is the so-called
Macdonald difference operator
, whose eigenfunctions are
symmetric Macdonald polynomials

Mac 98
;
Che 05
. The Macdonald functions of type
A_{1}
are labeled by spin-
\frac{j}{2}
representations, and can be expressed in terms of the basic hypergeometric series

P_{j}(X;q,t):=X^{j}\,{}_{2}\phi_{1}(q^{-2j},t^{2};q^{-2j+2}t^{-2};q^{2};q^{2}t^{-2}X^{-2})~.

(81)

They are acted on diagonally by the Macdonald difference operator, with eigenvalues

\pol(y)\cdot P_{j}(X;q,t)=(q^{j}t+q^{-j}t^{-1})P_{j}(X;q,t)~.

(82)

Under this basis, the actions of the other generators are

\displaystyle\pol(x)\cdot P_{j}(X;q,t)=

\displaystyle P_{j+1}(X;q,t)+\frac{\left(1-q^{2j}\right)\left(1-q^{2j-2}t^{4}\right)}{\left(1-q^{2j-2}t^{2}\right)\left(1-q^{2j}t^{2}\right)}P_{j-1}(X;q,t)~,

(83)

\displaystyle\pol(z)\cdot P_{j}(X;q,t)=

\displaystyle tq^{j+\frac{1}{2}}P_{j+1}(X;q,t)+t^{-1}q^{-j+\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\left(1-q^{2j}\right)\left(1-q^{2j-2}t^{4}\right)}{\left(1-q^{2j-2}t^{2}\right)\left(1-q^{2j}t^{2}\right)}P_{j-1}(X;q,t)~.

In fact, the Macdonald polynomials
P_{j}
form a basis for the ring
\mathscr{P}
over
\mathbb{C}_{q,t}
, so that the polynomial representation can be studied with the help of raising and lowering operators
KN 98
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{j}

\displaystyle:=x-q^{j-\frac{1}{2}}tz=X(q^{j}t^{-1}Y-q^{2j}t^{2})+X^{-1}(q^{j}tY^{-1}-q^{2j}t^{2})~,

(84)

\displaystyle\mathsf{L}_{j}

\displaystyle:=x-q^{-j-\frac{1}{2}}t^{-1}z=X(q^{-j}t^{-3}Y-q^{-2j}t^{-2})+X^{-1}(q^{-j}t^{-1}Y^{-1}-q^{-2j}t^{-2})~.

These operators relate adjacent Macdonald polynomials, respectively increasing or decreasing the value of
j
:

\displaystyle\pol(\mathsf{R}_{j})\cdot P_{j}(X;q,t)

\displaystyle=(1-q^{2j}t^{2})P_{j+1}(X;q,t)~,

(85)

\displaystyle\pol(\mathsf{L}_{j})\cdot P_{j}(X;q,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-q^{2j})(1-q^{2(j-1)}t^{4})}{q^{2j}t^{2}(q^{2(j-1)}t^{2}-1)}P_{j-1}(X;q,t)~.

(86)

See Figure
5
for a schematic diagram of this action.

Figure 5:
The action of raising and lowering operators on Macdonald polynomials

At
t=1
, this representation reduces to the pullback of the lift of
\mathscr{P}^{y_{1}=1}
in Proposition
C.6
so that Cherednik’s polynomial representation can be understood as its deformation from the symmetrized quantum torus to DAHA. Since the classical limit
(q=1)
of the Macdonald eigenvalues (
82
) is always
t+t^{-1}
, the support of the corresponding
A
-brane
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathbf{P}}
is given by

\mathbf{P}=\{y=\tilde{t}+\tilde{t}^{-1}~,\ z=\tilde{t}^{-1}x\}~.

(87)

While the parameter
t
in
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
coincides with the monodromy parameter
\tilde{t}
at the classical limit
(q=1)
(see (
56
)), we use
\tilde{t}
to specify the position of the brane because it is the geometric parameter of
\mathfrak{X}
.
Since it is of type
(A,B,A)
, it is happily a Lagrangian submanifold with respect to
\omega_{\mathfrak{X}}
for any value of
\hbar
or
q
.

To understand the brane
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathbf{P}}
for the polynomial representation
\mathscr{P}
of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
better, it is illuminating to consider its relation to the skein module.
The skein module of type
A_{1}

Tur 90
;
Prz 91
of an oriented 3-manifold
M_{3}
is defined as

\Sk(M_{3},\SU(2)):=\Sk(M_{3})=\frac{\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}](\text{isotopy classes of framed links in $M_{3}$})}{\Bigl(\raisebox{-7.5pt}{
