Appendix A
Glossary of symbols

As a general rule, single symbols in sans-serif type are used to denote lattices or other free
\mathbb{Z}
-modules. Words set in sans-serif type (e.g.
\text{$A$-}{\Brane}
) refer to categories. Italic symbols may be used for groups, algebras, or other classes of objects.
Calligraphic letters, such as
\mathcal{M}
or
\mathcal{B}
, denote objects which are moduli spaces or closely related to moduli spaces.
We reserve bold-face type for distinguished Lagrangian submanifolds of such moduli spaces, in particular for the support of branes (such as
\mathbf{F}
for a generic fiber of the Hitchin fibration).

Capital gothic letters (e.g.
\mathfrak{X}
for the target space) are used for objects equipped with the structure required by the topological
A
-model. As such,
\mathfrak{B}
denotes the
A
-brane associated to particular data; for instance,
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathbf{F}}
denotes a brane supported on the generic fiber of the Hitchin fibration. Note, though, that extra data on which the brane depends may be left implicit.

Script letters will denote modules of the algebra
\mathscr{O}^{q}(\mathfrak{X})
(precisely which algebra is intended will be clear from the context). We always suggestively use the same letter for a brane and its corresponding representation, so that (for example)
\mathscr{F}
will be identified with
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathbf{F}}
under the correspondence (
3
).

Occasionally, we cannot help using the same symbol for different notions due to the lack of letters. For instance, the symbol
T
is used for a maximal torus of a compact Lie group, a generator of DAHA, a two-torus and an element of
\SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
. However, we believe that the context is a sufficient guide.

In the following, we list notations of the paper.

•

{\mathcal{M}}_{H}
is a Hitchin moduli space (a moduli space of Higgs bundles),
\mathcal{M}_{C}
is a Coulomb branch,
\mathcal{M}_{\text{flat}}
is a moduli space of flat connections

•

\mathfrak{X}
is an affine variety over
\mathbb{C}
which admits the structure of a non-compact hyper-Kähler manifold. Depending on context, it may be
\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, or the moduli space of flat connections with coefficients in a complex Lie group.

•

G
is a compact gauge group,
T
is a Cartan subgroup of
G
, and
L
is a Levi subgroup of
G
.

•

G_{\mathbb{C}}
is the complexification of
G
,
T_{\mathbb{C}}
is a Cartan subgroup of
G_{\mathbb{C}}
,
B
is a Borel subgroup of
G_{\mathbb{C}}
, and
P
is a parabolic subgroup of
G_{\mathbb{C}}
.

•

\mathsf{R}
denotes a finite root system, and
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathsf{R}}:=\mathsf{R}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\delta
denotes the corresponding affine root system.
\mathsf{R}_{+}
denotes a set of positive roots.

•

\mathsf{Q}
and
\mathsf{Q}^{\vee}
denote the root and coroot lattice of
\mathsf{R}
, respectively. Similarly,
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{P}^{\vee}
denote the weight and coweight lattice of
\mathsf{R}
, respectively.
\mathsf{P}_{+}
denotes the set of dominant weights.

•

\mathsf{\Lambda}
and
\mathsf{\Lambda}^{\vee}
denote the character and cocharacter lattice, respectively. The center
Z(G)
and the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(G)
of
G
are given by quotients of lattices
Z(G)=\mathsf{P}^{\vee}/\mathsf{\Lambda}^{\vee}
and
\pi_{1}(G)=\mathsf{\Lambda}^{\vee}/\mathsf{Q}^{\vee}
, respectively. Their duals are
Z(G)^{\vee}=\mathsf{\Lambda}/\mathsf{Q}
and
\pi_{1}(G)^{\vee}=\mathsf{P}/\mathsf{\Lambda}
.

•

\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\}
denotes the set of simple roots and
\{\alpha_{1}^{\vee},\ldots,\alpha_{n}^{\vee}\}
denotes the set of simple coroots

•

W:=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})
is the Weyl group of
\mathsf{R}
where
s_{i}
denotes the reflection with respect to
\alpha_{i}
.

•

\alpha_{0}:=\delta-\theta
with
\theta
being the highest short root of
\mathsf{R}
so that
\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\}
is the set of simple roots of the affine root system
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathsf{R}}
.

•

\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{W}:=(s_{0},s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})
is the affine Weyl group for
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathsf{R}}
. Note that we have an isomorphism

\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{W}=W\ltimes\mathsf{t}(\mathsf{Q}^{\vee})

where
\mathsf{Q}^{\vee}
acts by affine translation
\mathsf{t}

\mathsf{t}(\alpha^{\vee})(\lambda)=\lambda-(\lambda,\alpha^{\vee})\delta~.

•

\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{W}^{e}:=W\ltimes\mathsf{t}(\mathsf{P}^{\vee})
denotes the
extended
affine Weyl group, which can be expressed as

\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{W}^{e}=\Pi\ltimes\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{W}

where
\Pi:=\mathsf{P}^{\vee}/\mathsf{Q}^{\vee}
acts faithfully on the set of simple roots
\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\}
. Hence, if
\pi_{r}(\alpha_{i})=\alpha_{j}
for
\pi_{r}\in\Pi
, then
\pi_{r}s_{i}\pi_{r}^{-1}=s_{j}
. (See Figure
20
.)

•

\BB(W)
,
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(W)
, and
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{W}
denote the double affine braid group, the double affine Hecke algebra and the double affine Weyl group associated to a Weyl group
W
of a root system.

Figure 20:
The action of the extended affine Weyl group
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{W}^{e}
of type
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A}_{2}
with generators
s_{0},s_{1},s_{2},\pi
. The reflection with respect to the axis
\alpha_{i}=0
is generated by
s_{i}
so that
s_{i}^{2}=1
. The braid relation can be seen as
D_{(i)}=s_{i+2}s_{i+1}s_{i+2}D=s_{i+1}s_{i+2}s_{i+1}D
, which is equivalent to the reflection with respect to the axis
\alpha_{i}=1
. The rotation of the triangle domain
D
around its center by 120
∘
is generated by
\pi
that satisfies
\pi^{3}=1
. One can also convince oneself
\pi s_{i}=s_{i+1}\pi
. Here the indices
i=0,1,2
are taken to be cyclic
i\cong i+3
. If we view the extended affine Weyl group as
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{W}^{e}=W\ltimes\mathsf{t}(\mathsf{P}^{\vee})
,
\mathsf{t}(\omega_{1})
and
\mathsf{t}(\omega_{2})
translate the domain
D
to
s_{0}s_{2}D
and
s_{0}s_{1}D
, respectively, where
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
are the fundamental weights of
A_{2}
.

Appendix B
Basics of DAHA

In this appendix, we will summarize the basics of double affine Hecke algebras. This appendix can be regarded as a concise review of
KJ 97
;
Mac 03
;
Che 05
;
Hai 06
.

B.1
DAHA

Let
t:=\{t_{0},\ldots,t_{n}\}
be a collection of formal variables associated to an affine Weyl group
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{W}
where we identify
t_{i}=t_{j}
if the reflections
s_{i}
and
s_{j}
are conjugate in
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{W}
. Note that we have at most two distinct
t
’s and, in particular, all the variables
t_{i}
are identical for simply-laced types (
A,D,E
). Let
q
be a formal variable and let
\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{m}},t_{0}^{\pm},\ldots,t_{n}^{\pm}]
be the ring of Laurent polynomials where
m
is the minimum positive integer satisfying
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{P}^{\vee})=\frac{1}{m}\mathbb{Z}
. Namely,
m=2
for type
D_{\textrm{even}}
;
m=1
for type
B_{\textrm{even}}
and type
C
; otherwise
m=|\Pi|
.
We consider a multiplicative system
M
in
\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{m}},t_{0}^{\pm},\ldots,t_{n}^{\pm}]
generated by elements of the form
(q^{\ell}t_{i}-q^{-\ell}t_{i}^{-1})
for any non-negative integer
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
with
i=0,\ldots,n
. We define the coefficient ring
\mathbb{C}_{q,t}
to be the localization (or formal “fraction”) of the ring
\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{m}},t_{0}^{\pm},\ldots,t_{n}^{\pm}]
at
M
:

\mathbb{C}_{q,t}=M^{-1}\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{m}},t_{0}^{\pm},\ldots,t_{n}^{\pm}]~.

Moreover, we define
\mathbb{C}_{t}:=\mathbb{C}_{q,t}/(q^{\frac{1}{m}}-1)
and
\mathbb{C}_{q}:=\mathbb{C}_{q,t}/(t_{0}-1,\ldots,t_{n}-1)
.

The DAHA
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(W)
associated to a Lie group
G
is the
\mathbb{C}_{q,t}
-algebra generated by elements
T_{0},\ldots,T_{n},\Pi,X^{\mathsf{P}}
with relations

1.

(braid relation)
T_{i}T_{j}T_{i}\cdots=T_{j}T_{i}T_{j}\cdots
with
m_{ij}
terms on each side. (
m_{ij}
is defined via the Coxeter relations
(s_{i}s_{j})^{m_{ij}}=1
in the Weyl group
W
.)

2.

(quadratic relation)

(T_{i}-t_{i})(T_{i}+t_{i}^{-1})=0~.

(209)

3.

(first affinization)
\pi_{r}T_{i}\pi_{r}^{-1}=T_{j}
if
\pi_{r}(\alpha_{i})=\alpha_{j}
.

4.

(second affinization) For
i=0,\ldots,n
,

\begin{array}[]{ll}T_{i}X^{\mu}=X^{\mu}T_{i}&\qquad\textrm{if}\quad(\mu,\alpha_{i}^{\vee})=0\\
T_{i}X^{\mu}=X^{s_{i}(\mu)}T_{i}^{-1}&\qquad\textrm{if}\quad(\mu,\alpha_{i}^{\vee})=1\end{array}

(210)

where
\alpha_{0}^{\vee}:=-\theta^{\vee}
.

5.

\pi_{r}X^{\mu}\pi_{r}^{-1}=X^{\pi_{r}(\mu)}
.

The deformation parameters
t_{i}
manifestly appear in the quadratic relation (
209
) whereas
the other deformation parameter
q
implicitly shows up in the second relation of (
210
) via
q=X^{\delta}
. The most important part is
i=0
because
s_{0}(\mu)=\mu-\alpha_{0}=\mu+\theta-\delta
for
(\mu,\alpha^{\vee}_{0})=1
. In this case, the relation becomes

T_{0}X^{\mu}=X^{s_{0}(\mu)}T_{0}^{-1}=X^{\mu-\alpha_{0}}T_{0}^{-1}=q^{-1}X^{\mu+\theta}T_{0}^{-1}~.

As the name suggests, DAHA
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(W)
contains two affine Hecke algebras as subalgebras:

•

(affine Hecke algebra for the root system
\mathsf{R}^{\vee}
)
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{H}^{X}(W):=\mathbb{C}_{t}(T_{1},\ldots,T_{n},X^{\mathsf{P}})

•

(affine Hecke algebra for the root system
\mathsf{R}
)
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{H}^{Y}(W):=\mathbb{C}_{t}(T_{0},T_{1},\ldots,T_{n},\Pi)

where both contain finite Hecke algebra
H(W):=\mathbb{C}_{t}(T_{1},\ldots,T_{n})
.
Indeed, the first representation
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{H}^{X}(W)
is called the
Bernstein presentation
whereas the second one is called the
Coxeter presentation
. The second one
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{H}^{Y}(W)
can also be expressed in the Bernstein presentation by using an isomorphism as vector spaces

H(W)\otimes\mathbb{C}[Y^{\mathsf{P}^{\vee}}]\xrightarrow{\sim}H^{Y}(W)~,

where the affine translation in
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{H}^{Y}(W)
is generated by
Y
:

\begin{array}[]{ll}Y^{\lambda}:=T_{\mathsf{t}(\lambda)}&\textrm{if}\quad\lambda\in\mathsf{P}^{\vee}_{+}\cr Y^{\lambda}:=Y^{\mu}(Y^{\nu})^{-1}&\textrm{if}\quad\lambda=\mu-\nu\quad\mu,\nu\in\mathsf{P}^{\vee}_{+}~.\end{array}

(211)

B.1.1
Double affine braid group and double affine Weyl group

The double affine braid group
\BB(W)
is a group generated by elements
T_{0},\ldots,T_{n},\Pi,X^{\mathsf{P}}
only with the relations 1,3,4,5 above (without the relation 2). In other words, DAHA can be understood as the
\mathbb{C}_{q,t}
-group algebra of
\BB(W)
with the quadratic relation (
209
)

\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(W)=\mathbb{C}_{q,t}[\BB(W)]/((T_{i}-t_{i})(T_{i}+t_{i}^{-1}))~.

The double affine Weyl group
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{W}
is
\BB(W)
with the quadratic relations
T_{i}^{2}=1
. Therefore, its
\mathbb{C}_{q}
-group algebra is the
t=1
limit of DAHA
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(W)
, which is also introduced at the beginning of §
2

\mathbb{C}_{q}[\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{W}]=\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{t=1}(W)~.

In the Bernstein presentation, the generators
X
and
Y
form the Heisenberg group as a subgroup of
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{W}
with the relation

X^{\mu}Y^{\lambda}=q^{(\mu,\lambda)}Y^{\lambda}X^{\mu},\qquad\textrm{for}\quad\mu\in\mathsf{P},\ \lambda\in\mathsf{P}^{\vee}~.

B.1.2
PBW theorem for DAHA

First of all, the following Poincaré-Birkhoff-Witt theorem for DAHA plays a very important role in the representation theory.
Every element
h\in\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(W)
can be
uniquely
written in the form

h=\sum_{r\in\Pi,w\in\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{W},\mu\in\mathsf{P}}a_{\mu,w,r}(h)~X^{\mu}T_{w}\pi_{r}\qquad a_{\mu,w,r}(h)\in\mathbb{C}_{q,t}

in the Coxeter presentation for
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{H}^{Y}(W)
, or

h=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathsf{P}^{\vee},w\in W,\mu\in\mathsf{P}}b_{\mu,w,\lambda}(h)~X^{\mu}T_{w}Y^{\lambda}\qquad b_{\mu,w,\lambda}(h)\in\mathbb{C}_{q,t}

(212)

in the Bernstein presentation for
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{H}^{Y}(W)
.

B.1.3
Spherical subalgebra

For
w\in W
, let
t_{w}:=t_{i_{1}}\cdots t_{i_{k}}
, where
w=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{k}}
is a reduced decomposition. Then, it is well-defined.
We define an element in the group algebra
\mathbb{C}_{t}[W]

\mathbf{e}:=\frac{\sum_{w\in W}t_{w}T_{w}}{\sum_{w\in W}(t_{w})^{2}}~.

(213)

Then, we have
T_{i}\mathbf{e}=t_{i}\mathbf{e}
, and it is moreover an idempotent
\mathbf{e}^{2}=\mathbf{e}
. Subsequently,
we can define the spherical subalgebra as

S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(W):=\mathbf{e}\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(W)\mathbf{e}\subset\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(W)
, called
spherical DAHA
. Roughly speaking, the spherical DAHA can be understood as the subalgebra “averaged over” the Weyl group symmetry.

At
t=1=q
, the spherical DAHA becomes commutative

S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(W)\Big|_{t=1=q}=\mathbb{C}[T_{\mathbb{C}}\times T_{\mathbb{C}}]^{W}~.

Indeed, it is isomorphic to the coordinate ring of the moduli space

\mathcal{M}_{\text{flat}}(T^{2},G_{\mathbb{C}})=\frac{T_{\mathbb{C}}\times T_{\mathbb{C}}}{W}

(214)

of flat
G_{\mathbb{C}}
-connections on a torus
Obl04a
.

B.1.4
Braid group and
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
action

The braid group on three strands is given by
B_{3}=(\tau_{\pm}\text{ : }\tau_{+}\tau^{-1}_{-}\tau_{+}=\tau^{-1}_{-}\tau_{+}\tau^{-1}_{-})
, and the relation to
\SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
is given by the short exact sequence

0\to\mathbb{Z}\to B_{3}\to\SL(2,\mathbb{Z})\to 1

(215)

where the kernel of the projection
B_{3}\to\SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
is
\mathbb{Z}
generated by
\left(\tau_{+}\tau_{-}^{-1}\tau_{+}\right)^{4}
.

DAHA
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(W)
receives an action of the braid group on three strands
B_{3}=(\tau_{\pm}\text{ : }\tau_{+}\tau^{-1}_{-}\tau_{+}=\tau^{-1}_{-}\tau_{+}\tau^{-1}_{-})
.
To see the action explicitly, let
\omega_{i}
,
\omega_{i}^{\vee}
(
i=1,\dots,n
) denote the fundamental weights and coweights respectively, and we define
X_{i}:=X^{\omega_{i}}
and
Y_{i}=Y^{\omega_{i}^{\vee}}
as the corresponding generators of
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(W)
in the Bernstein presentation. Then, the action reads

\tau_{+}:\begin{cases}X^{\mu}\mapsto X^{\mu}\\
T_{j}\mapsto T_{j}\\
Y_{i}\mapsto X_{i}Y_{i}q^{-(\omega_{i},\omega_{i})}\end{cases}~,\qquad\qquad\tau_{-}:\begin{cases}X_{i}\mapsto Y_{i}X_{i}q^{(\omega_{i},\omega_{i})}\\
T_{j}\mapsto T_{j}\\
Y^{\lambda}\mapsto Y^{\lambda}\end{cases}~.

(216)

The element
\sigma=\tau_{+}\tau_{-}^{-1}\tau_{+}=\tau_{-}^{-1}\tau_{+}\tau_{-}^{-1}
maps the generators as

\sigma:\begin{cases}X^{\mu}\mapsto Y^{-\mu^{\vee}}\\
T_{j}\mapsto T_{j}\\
Y^{\lambda^{\vee}}\mapsto T_{w_{\circ}}^{-1}X^{w_{\circ}(\lambda)}T_{w_{\circ}}\end{cases}~,\qquad\qquad\sigma^{2}:\begin{cases}X^{\mu}\mapsto T_{w_{\circ}}^{-1}X^{-w_{\circ}(\mu)}T_{w_{\circ}}\\
T_{j}\mapsto T_{j}\\
Y^{\lambda}\mapsto T_{w_{\circ}}^{-1}Y^{-w_{\circ}(\lambda)}T_{w_{\circ}}\end{cases}~,

(217)

where
w_{\circ}
is the longest element of the Weyl group
W
.
Moreover,
\sigma^{4}
acts as the conjugation by
T_{w_{\circ}}^{2}
, namely
\sigma^{4}(x)=T_{w_{\circ}}^{-2}(x)T_{w_{\circ}}^{2}
for any
x\in\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(W)
.

Since
T_{w}
acts on a generator of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(W)
as the multiplication by
t_{w}
, the action of
\sigma^{4}
is trivial on the spherical subalgebra
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(W)
in general:

\sigma^{4}\Big|_{S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(W)}\cong\textrm{id}_{S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(W)}~.

Therefore, the
B_{3}
action factors through
\SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
on
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(W)
, and each element corresponds to the following matrix element on
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(W)

17
17

17

Although we follow the notation of
Che 05
for the transformations
\tau_{\pm}
on the generators of DAHA here, we change matrix assignments (
218
) to
\tau_{\pm}
from
Che 05
.
:

\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}\leftrightarrow\tau_{+}~,\quad\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\leftrightarrow\tau_{-}~,\quad\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\leftrightarrow\sigma~.

(218)

B.1.5
Polynomial representation of DAHA

The most basic representation of DAHA, first studied by Cherednik, is called
the polynomial representation

\displaystyle\pol:\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(W)

\displaystyle\to\End(\mathbb{C}_{q,t}[X^{\mathsf{P}}]);

(219)

\displaystyle T_{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto t_{i}s_{i}+(t_{i}-t_{i}^{-1})\frac{s_{i}-1}{X^{\alpha_{i}}-1}\qquad\mathrm{for}\ i=0,\ldots,n

\displaystyle\pi_{r}

\displaystyle\mapsto\pi_{r}\qquad\mathrm{for}\ \pi_{r}\in\Pi~.

Here we denote the group algebra of the weight lattice
\mathsf{P}
by
\mathbb{C}_{q,t}[X^{\mathsf{P}}]
, which contains the group algebra
\mathbb{C}_{t}[X^{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathsf{P}}}]
of the affine weight lattice
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathsf{P}}:=\mathsf{P}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\delta
as a subalgebra by setting
X^{\lambda+r\delta}:=q^{r}X^{\lambda}
. Hence,
X^{\mu}
act on
\mathbb{C}_{q,t}[X^{\mathsf{P}}]
by multiplication, and the action of the extended affine Weyl group
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{W}^{e}
on
\mathbb{C}_{q,t}[X^{\mathsf{P}}]
, which appears as
s_{i}
in (
219
), is given by

w(X^{\mu}):=X^{w(\mu)}=q^{-(v(\mu),\lambda)}X^{v(\mu)}~,

where we write an element
w\in\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{W}^{e}
as
w=\mathsf{t}(\lambda)v
with
\lambda\in\mathsf{P}^{\vee}
,
v\in W
. Note that the polynomial representation is faithful.

Non-symmetric Macdonald polynomials

We define an involution
f\mapsto f^{*}
for
f=\sum_{\lambda}f_{\lambda}(q,t)X^{\lambda}\in\mathbb{C}_{q,t}[X]
as

f^{*}=\sum_{\lambda}f_{\lambda}(q^{-1},t^{-1})X^{-\lambda}~.

We also define the weight function

\Xi:=\prod_{\alpha\in\mathsf{R}_{+}}\frac{(X^{\alpha};q^{2})_{\infty}(q^{2}X^{-\alpha};q^{2})_{\infty}}{(q^{2c_{\alpha}}X^{\alpha};q^{2})_{\infty}(q^{2c_{\alpha}+2}X^{-\alpha};q^{2})_{\infty}}~.

Note that
c_{\alpha}
relates
q
and
t=\{t_{0},\dots,t_{n}\}
via
q^{c_{\alpha}}=t_{\alpha}
where

c_{\alpha}=c_{\beta}
if
\alpha
and
\beta
are in the same
W
-orbit,
i.e.
there exists
w\in W
such that
w(\alpha)=\beta
. In particular, there is only one parameter
c
for simply-laced ADE types.
Then, the product
\textbf{(}\ ,\ \textbf{)}
on
\mathbb{C}_{q,t}[X]
is defined as the
constant term
(C.T.) of
fg^{*}\Xi

\textbf{(}f,g\textbf{)}=\textrm{C.T.}(fg^{*}\Xi)~,

which can be taken by the integral

{\rm C.T.}\ h(X):=\oint_{|X_{1}|=1}\frac{dX_{1}}{X_{1}}\ldots\oint_{|X_{\rk(G)}|=1}\frac{dX_{\rk(G)}}{X_{\rk(G)}}~h(X)~.

Using the product
\textbf{(}\ ,\ \textbf{)}
, one can show the existence and uniqueness of
non-symmetric Macdonald polynomials

E_{\lambda}(X;q,t)
as follows.

For each
\lambda\in\mathsf{P}
, there is a unique polynomial
E_{\lambda}\in\mathbb{C}_{q,t}[X]
such that

•

E_{\lambda}=X^{\lambda}+\textrm{lower terms}~,

•

\textbf{(}E_{\lambda},X^{\mu}\textbf{)}=0\quad\textrm{for}\ {}^{\forall}\mu<\lambda~.

Under the polynomial representation,
these are eigenfunctions of the
Y
-operators consequently as

\displaystyle\pol(f(Y))\cdot E_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=f(q^{-2\lambda-2\rho_{c}(\lambda)})E_{\lambda}

(220)

where

\rho_{c}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha\in\mathsf{R}_{+}}\eta((\lambda,\alpha))c_{\alpha}\alpha\qquad\text{with}\qquad\eta(x)=\begin{cases}1&\textrm{if}\ x>0\\
-1&\textrm{if}\ x\leq 0\end{cases}~.

Symmetric Macdonald polynomials

We define another involution
f\mapsto\overline{f}
for
f=\sum_{\lambda}f_{\lambda}(q,t)X^{\lambda}\in\mathbb{C}_{q,t}[X]
as

\overline{f}=\sum_{\lambda}f_{\lambda}(q,t)X^{-\lambda}~.

(221)

We also define another product
(
Mac 98
, §VI.9)
on
\mathbb{C}_{q,t}[X]
as

\langle f,g\rangle=\frac{1}{|W|}\textrm{C.T.}(f\overline{g}\,\Upsilon)\qquad\textrm{where}\qquad\Upsilon=\prod_{\alpha\in\mathsf{R}}\frac{(X^{\alpha};q^{2})_{\infty}}{(t_{\alpha}^{2}X^{\alpha};q^{2})_{\infty}}~.

(222)

Since
\Upsilon=\overline{\Upsilon}
, it is symmetric
\langle f,g\rangle=\langle g,f\rangle
.
Furthermore, for a dominant weight
\lambda\in\mathsf{P}_{+}
, we define
monomial symmetric functions

m_{\lambda}=\sum_{\mu\in W(\lambda)}X^{\mu}~.

Using these data, one can show the existence and uniqueness of
symmetric Macdonald polynomials

P_{\lambda}(X;q,t)
as follows.

For each dominant weight
\lambda\in\mathsf{P}_{+}
, there is a unique function
P_{\lambda}\in\mathbb{C}_{q,t}[X]^{W}
such that

•

P_{\lambda}=m_{\lambda}+\textrm{lower terms}~,

•

\langle P_{\lambda},m_{\mu}\rangle=0\quad\textrm{for}\ {}^{\forall}\mu\in\mathsf{P}_{+}
such that
\mu<\lambda~,

Under the product, the symmetric Macdonald polynomials are orthogonal. In the case of type
A_{N-1}
, the norm is explicitly given by

\langle P_{\lambda},P_{\lambda^{\prime}}\rangle=g_{\lambda}\delta_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}~,\qquad g_{\lambda}(q,t)=\prod_{(i,j)\in\lambda}\frac{1-q^{2(\lambda_{i}-j+1)}t^{2(\lambda^{T}_{j}-i)}}{1-q^{2(\lambda_{i}-j)}t^{2(\lambda^{T}_{j}-i+1)}}\frac{1-t^{2(N-i+1)}q^{2(j-1)}}{1-t^{2(N-i)}q^{2j}}~,

(223)

where we express a Young diagram
\lambda=\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{N-1}
.

Let
f
be a symmetric polynomial
f\in\mathbb{C}_{q,t}[Y^{\mathsf{P}^{\vee}}]^{W}
. Then, under the polynomial representation, they are eigenfunctions

\pol(f(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}))\cdot P_{\lambda}=f(q^{-2\lambda-2\rho_{c}})P_{\lambda}~,

(224)

where
\rho_{c}
is the formal expression

\rho_{c}:=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha\in\mathsf{R}_{+}}c_{\alpha}\alpha~.

Note that, for type ADE, it simplifies to
q^{-2\lambda-2\rho_{c}}=q^{-2\lambda}t^{-2\rho}
where
\rho
is the Weyl vector.
The non-symmetric and symmetric Macdonald polynomials are related by the idempotent (
213
)

P_{\lambda}=\Big(\sum_{w\in W}(t_{w})^{2}\Big)\,\mathbf{e}E_{\lambda}~.

B.1.6
Symmetric bilinear form

There exists an anti-involution
\phi:\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(W)\to\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(W)
fixing
q,t
such that

\phi:\begin{cases}X^{\mu}\mapsto Y^{-\mu^{\vee}}\\
T_{w}\mapsto T_{w^{-1}}\\
Y^{\lambda^{\vee}}\mapsto X^{-\lambda}\end{cases}

It is easy to see
\phi^{2}=\id
.
If we restrict the action of
\phi
to the spherical subalgebra
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(W)
, then it is the same as
\sigma
in (
217
)

\phi\Big|_{S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(W)}\cong\sigma\Big|_{S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(W)}~.

(225)

We define the evaluation map
\theta:\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(W)\to\mathbb{C}_{q,t}
as

\theta(h):=\pol(h)\cdot 1\Big|_{X\mapsto q^{-2\rho_{c}}}

(226)

Then, one can define a symmetric
\mathbb{C}_{q,t}
-bilinear form
\textbf{[}\ ,\ \textbf{]}\,.:\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(W)\times\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(W)\to\mathbb{C}_{q,t}
as

\textbf{[}h,h^{\prime}\textbf{]}:=\theta(\phi(h)h^{\prime})~.

(227)

In particular, for
f,g\in\mathbb{C}_{q,t}[X]
, we have

\textbf{[}f,g\textbf{]}=\pol(f(Y^{-1}))\cdot g\Big|_{X\mapsto q^{-2\rho_{c}}}~.

(228)

For instance, when both are symmetric Macdonald polynomials,
f=P_{\lambda}(X)
,
g=P_{\mu}(X)
, (
224
) tells us

\textbf{[}P_{\lambda}(X),P_{\mu}(X)\textbf{]}=P_{\lambda}(q^{2\mu+2\rho_{c}})P_{\mu}(q^{-2\rho_{c}})~,

(229)

which is the refined Chern-Simons invariants of the Hopf link
AS 15
.
In fact, using the PBW theorem, one can bring
\phi(h)h^{\prime}
into the form (
212
)

\phi(h)h^{\prime}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathsf{P}^{\vee},w\in W,\mu\in\mathsf{P}}b_{\mu,w,\lambda}(\phi(h)h^{\prime})~X^{\mu}T_{w}Y^{\lambda}~.

Then, the bilinear from
\textbf{[}\ ,\ \textbf{]}
is indeed expressed by

\textbf{[}h,h^{\prime}\textbf{]}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathsf{P}^{\vee},w\in W,\mu\in\mathsf{P}}b_{\mu,w,\lambda}(\phi(h)h^{\prime})~q^{-2(\mu,\rho_{c})}\,t_{w}\,q^{2(\lambda,\rho_{c})}~.

Since
\phi
and the generators
\tau_{+},\tau_{-}\in\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
obey the relation
\phi\tau_{-}\phi=\tau_{+}
, we have

\textbf{[}\tau_{+}(h),h^{\prime}\textbf{]}=\textbf{[}h,\tau_{-}(h^{\prime})\textbf{]}~.

(230)

B.1.7
Degenerations

There is a two-step degeneration of DAHA:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\textrm{DAHA}&&\textrm{trigonometric}&&\textrm{rational}\\
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(W)&\rightsquigarrow&\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{tri}}(W)&\rightsquigarrow&\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{rat}}(W)\end{array}

Trigonometric degeneration

Let
c_{i}
,
i=0,\dots,n
be formal variables such that
c_{i}=c_{j}
whenever
s_{i}
and
s_{j}
are conjugate. We will also take commuting variables
\hat{x}_{1},\dots,\hat{x}_{n}
and, for
\mu\in\mathsf{P}
, we will denote

\hat{x}_{\mu}:=\sum(\mu,\alpha_{j}^{\vee})\hat{x}_{j}~.

The extended affine Weyl group
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{W}^{e}
acts on the space
\mathbb{C}[c,\hbar][\hat{x}_{1},\dots,\hat{x}_{n}]
where
s_{1},\dots,s_{n}
acts in the standard way
s_{i}(\hat{x}_{\mu})=\hat{x}_{s_{i}(\mu)}
for
i=1,\dots,n
. On the other hand, the affine translation involves
\hbar
as
\mathsf{t}(\lambda)(\hat{x}_{\mu})=\hat{x}_{\mu}-(\mu,\lambda)\hbar
for
\lambda\in\mathsf{P}^{\vee}
.

The
trigonometric Cherednik algebra
(a.k.a.
graded Cherednik algebra

OY 16
or
degenerate double affine Hecke algebra

(
Che 05
, §1.6)
also
SV 13
),
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{tri}}(W)
is the
\mathbb{C}[c,\hbar]
-algebra generated by the extended affine Weyl group
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{W}^{e}
and pairwise commuting variables
\hat{x}_{1},\dots,\hat{x}_{n}
, subject to the following relations:

\displaystyle s_{i}\hat{x}_{\mu}-\hat{x}_{s_{i}(\mu)}s_{i}

\displaystyle=-c_{i}(\mu,\alpha_{i}^{\vee}),\qquad\textrm{for}\ i=1,\ldots,n

(231)

\displaystyle s_{0}\hat{x}_{\mu}-s_{0}(\hat{x}_{\mu})s_{0}

\displaystyle=c_{0}(\mu,\theta^{\vee}),

\displaystyle\pi_{r}\hat{x}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\hat{x}_{\pi_{r}(\mu)}\pi_{r}.

This algebra can be obtained by taking the leading relation in the
\beta
expansion from DAHA
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(W)
as in Appendix
B.2.3
.

Since the variables
Y,\hat{x}
are not symmetric, the algebra
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{tri}}(W)
admits two polynomial representations.
One is called the
differential polynomial representation
on the group algebra
\mathbb{C}[c,\hbar][Y^{\mathsf{P}^{\vee}}]
. The generators
Y
and
w\in W
act naturally, and
\hat{x}_{\mu}
acts via the

trigonometric differential Dunkl operator

D^{\textrm{tri}}_{\mu}:=\hbar\partial_{\mu}+\sum_{\alpha\in\mathsf{R}_{+}}\frac{c_{\alpha}(\mu,\alpha^{\vee})}{1-Y^{-\alpha^{\vee}}}(\textrm{id}-s_{\alpha})-(\mu,\rho_{c}^{\vee})~.

Here the derivative
\partial_{\mu}
acts on
\mathbb{C}[c,\hbar][Y]
of the weight lattice
\mathsf{P}^{\vee}
as

\partial_{\mu}(Y^{\lambda})=(\mu,\lambda)Y^{\lambda}~,

and
\rho_{c}^{\vee}
is the formal expression

\rho_{c}^{\vee}:=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha\in\mathsf{R}_{+}}c_{\alpha}\alpha^{\vee}~.

The other is called the
difference-rational polynomial representation
on the group algebra
\mathbb{C}[c,\hbar][\hat{x}]
. The generator
\hat{x}
acts by multiplication.
By defining the
Demazure-Lusztig operators

S_{i}:=s_{i}+\frac{c_{i}}{\hat{x}_{\alpha_{i}}}(s_{i}-\textrm{id})~

for
i=0,1,\dots,n
, the action of the extended affine Weyl group
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{W}^{e}
is given by
S_{w}:=\pi_{r}S_{i_{1}}\cdots S_{i_{j}}
for
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{W}^{e}\ni w=\pi_{r}s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{j}}
a reduced expression.

Rational degeneration

The
rational Cherednik algebra

\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{rat}}(W)

BEG 03
is the
\mathbb{C}[c,\hbar]
-algebra generated by
\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{P}]
,
\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{P}^{\vee}]
and
W
subject to the relations

w\hat{x}=w(\hat{x})w~,\quad w\hat{y}=w(\hat{y})w~,\quad[\hat{y},\hat{x}]=\hbar(\hat{x},\hat{y})-\sum_{\alpha\in\mathsf{R}_{+}}c_{\alpha}(\alpha,\hat{y})(\hat{x},\alpha^{\vee})s_{\alpha}

for
w\in W
,
\hat{x}\in\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{P}]
and
\hat{y}\in\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{P}^{\vee}]
. This algebra can be obtained from trigonometric Cherednik algebra
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{tri}}(W)
by taking the leading relation in the
\beta
expansion as in Appendix
B.2.4
.

The rational Cherednik algebra
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{rat}}(W)
admits a polynomial representation on the space
\mathbb{C}[c,\hbar][\mathsf{P}]
where
\hat{x}
and
w
act in the standard way, and
\hat{y}
is assigned to the
rational Dunkl operator

D^{\textrm{rat}}_{\hat{y}}:=\hbar\partial_{\hat{y}}-\sum_{\alpha\in\mathsf{R}_{+}}c_{\alpha}\frac{(\alpha,y)}{\alpha}(\textrm{id}-s_{\alpha})~,

(232)

where
\partial_{\hat{y}}(\hat{x})=(\hat{x},\hat{y})
,
\hat{x}\in\mathsf{P}
.

Spherical subalgebras

We can also define spherical subalgebras of both trigonometric and rational Cherednik algebras by

S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{tri}}(W):=\mathbf{e}\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{tri}}(W)\mathbf{e}~,\qquad S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{rat}}(W):=\mathbf{e}\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{rat}}(W)\mathbf{e}~,

where
\mathbf{e}
now is the trivial idempotent of the group
W

\mathbf{e}=\frac{1}{|W|}\sum_{w\in W}w~.

At
c=0=\hbar
, the spherical subalgebras of trigonometric and rational Cherednik algebras become the commutative rings

S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{tri}}(W)\Big|_{c=0=\hbar}=\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{C}}\times T_{\mathbb{C}}]^{W}~,\qquad S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{rat}}(W)\Big|_{c=0=\hbar}=\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{C}}\times\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{C}}]^{W}~.

(233)

Therefore, they are the coordinate rings of the hyper-Kähler manifolds

\frac{\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{C}}\times T_{\mathbb{C}}}{W}~,\quad\textrm{and}\quad\frac{\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{C}}\times\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{C}}}{W}~,

respectively
BFM 05
where their complex structures inherit from their elliptic origin (
214
).

B.2
DAHA of type
A_{1}

Now we will study DAHA
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}:=\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})
of type
A_{1}
more in detail. In the case of type
A_{1}
, the relations
1,\dots,5
in Appendix
B.1
amount to

\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}=\mathbb{C}_{q,t}\bigl[T^{\pm 1},X^{\pm 1},\pi^{\pm 1}\bigr]\bigg/\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}TXT=X^{-1}~,&\pi X\pi^{-1}=qX^{-1}~,\\
\pi^{2}=1~,&(T-t)(T+t^{-1})=0\end{array}\right\}.

Here
T
and
X
generate affine Hecke algebra
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{H}^{X}
in the Bernstein presentation whereas
T
and
\pi
generate the other affine Hecke algebra
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{H}^{Y}
in the Coxeter presentation. One can write
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{H}^{Y}
in the Bernstein presentation by introducing
Y=\pi T
, yielding another representation of
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}

\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}=\mathbb{C}_{q,t}\bigl[T^{\pm 1},X^{\pm 1},Y^{\pm 1}\bigr]\bigg/\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}TXT=X^{-1}~,&Y^{-1}X^{-1}YXT^{2}=q^{-1}~,\\
TY^{-1}T=Y~,&(T-t)(T+t^{-1})=0\end{array}\right\}.

(234)

In this representation, the topological interpretation using the punctured torus becomes apparent as we have seen in Appendix
2.2
.

B.2.1
Polynomial representation

The
polynomial representation
of DAHA of type
A_{1}

\pol:\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}\rightarrow\textrm{End}(\mathbb{C}_{q,t}[X^{\pm}])

(235)

is explicitly given by

T\mapsto ts+\frac{t-t^{-1}}{X^{2}-1}(s-1)~,\quad\pi\mapsto s\varpi~,\quad X\mapsto X~,\quad Y\mapsto s\varpi T~,

where
s
is the reflection
s(X)=X^{-1}
, and
\varpi
is the shift operator
\varpi(X)=qX
.

To study the polynomial representation, we introduce a basis of
\mathbb{C}_{q,t}[X^{\pm}]
spanned by
non-symmetric Macdonald polynomials
of type
A_{1}

E_{j}(X;q,t)=\begin{cases}X^{j}\,{}_{2}\phi_{1}(q^{-2j+2},t^{2};q^{-2j+2}t^{-2};q^{2};q^{2}t^{-2}X^{-2})&\textrm{if}\ j>0\\
X^{-j}\,{}_{2}\phi_{1}(q^{2j},t^{2};q^{2j}t^{-2};q^{2};t^{-2}X^{2})&\textrm{if}\ j\leq 0\end{cases}~,

where
{}_{2}\phi_{1}(a_{1},a_{2};b_{1};q;X)
is the basic hypergeometric series.
As we have seen in (
220
), they are eigenfunctions of the
Y
-operator under the polynomial representation

\pol(Y)E_{j}=\begin{cases}q^{-j}t^{-1}E_{j}&\textrm{if}\ j>0\\
q^{j}tE_{j}&\textrm{if}\ j\leq 0\end{cases}~.

(236)

If we introduce a total ordering on the monomials

1\prec X\prec X^{-1}\prec X^{2}\prec X^{-2}\prec X^{3}\prec X^{-3}\prec\cdots~,

then they are subject to the condition

E_{j}(X;q,t)=X^{j}+\textrm{lower order terms}~.

The coefficients of the polynomials
E_{j}
are rational functions of
q
and
t
in general.

For generic
q
and
t
, the non-symmetric Macdonald polynomials
E_{j}
are recursively determined by using the
intertwining
(raising) operators
(
Che 05
, §2.6.2)
.
Explicitly, they are defined as

\mathsf{A}:=XT~,\qquad\mathsf{B}:=t\Bigl(T+\frac{t-t^{-1}}{Y^{-2}-1}\Bigr)~,

and they act on non-symmetric Macdonald polynomials as

\displaystyle\pol(\mathsf{A})\cdot E_{j}

\displaystyle=t^{-\textrm{sign}(j)}E_{1-j}~,

(237)

\displaystyle\pol(\mathsf{B})\cdot E_{j}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}E_{-j}&\textrm{for}\ j>0\\
\frac{t(1-q^{-2j})(1-q^{-2j}t^{4})}{(1-q^{-2j}t^{2})^{2}}E_{-j}&\textrm{for}\ j\leq 0\end{cases}~.

In fact, these intertwining operators
\mathsf{A},\mathsf{B}
provide an inductive procedure for calculating
the non-symmetric polynomials

1=E_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{A}}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}E_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{B}}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}E_{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{A}}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}E_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{B}}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}E_{-2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{A}}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}\cdots.

(238)

Because of
t\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, the intertwining operator
\mathsf{A}
is always invertible. Hence, in order to classify finite-dimensional representations, one has to understand when
\mathsf{B}
is not invertible.
Hence, as we have seen in (
86
) and (
103
), finite-dimensional modules arise when the factor

\frac{t(1-q^{-2j})(1-q^{-j}t^{2})(1+q^{-j}t^{2})}{(1-q^{-2j}t^{2})^{2}}

(239)

vanishes where
j\leq 0
. Then, it is easy to see that there is a one-to-one correspondence between finite-dimensional modules of
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
and
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
because the shortening conditions for (
103
) and (
239
) are equivalent. In other words, for finite-dimensional representations, one can easily verify the consequence of the Morita equivalence (
79
) explicitly by the action of the intertwining operators. Therefore, the classification of the finite-dimensional representations of
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}

(
Che 05
, §2.8)
is in one-to-one correspondence with that of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
in §
2.6
.

The symmetric Macdonald polynomials
P_{j}
(
81
) for
j\geq 0
can be obtained by symmetrizing
E_{\pm j}
with the idempotent
\mathbf{e}=(T+t^{-1})/(t+t^{-1})

\displaystyle P_{j}(X;q,t)=\begin{cases}(1+t^{2})\,\mathbf{e}E_{j}(X;q,t)~,\\
\frac{(1+t^{2})(1-q^{2j}t^{2})}{(1-q^{2j}t^{4})}\,\mathbf{e}E_{-j}(X;q,t)~,\end{cases}

(240)

or by the combination of
E_{j}
and
E_{-j}

P_{j}(X;q,t)=E_{-j}(X;q,t)+\frac{t^{2}(1-q^{2j})}{1-q^{2j}t^{2}}E_{j}(X;q,t)~.

(241)

On the other hand, non-symmetric Macdonald polynomials
E_{j}
can also be obtained from symmetric ones
P_{j}
as follows. If one expresses

P_{j}=\sum_{i=0}^{j}c_{j,i}(q,t)(X^{i}+X^{-i})~,

then

E_{j}=\sum_{i=-j}^{j}d_{j,i}(q,t)X^{i}\qquad\textrm{where}\qquad d_{j,i}(q,t):=\frac{1-q^{i+j}}{q^{i-j}-q^{i+j}}c_{j,i}(q,t)~.

Furthermore, one can easily determine
E_{-j}
for
j>0
from
P_{j}
by (
241
). Hence, the polynomial representations for DAHA
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
(
235
) and spherical DAHA
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
(
80
) are equivalent, which follows from the Morita equivalence (
79
).

To conclude this subsection, let us analyze how Macdonald polynomials are transformed under
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
transformation. In fact, under the
\tau_{-}
transformation (
48
), non-symmetric Macdonald polynomials are subject to

\tau_{-}(E_{j})=q^{-\frac{j^{2}}{2}}t^{-|j|}E_{j}~,

(242)

for
j\in\mathbb{Z}
. This can be proved by the induction in the sequence (
238
). For
j=0
, it is easy to see that
\tau_{-}(1)=1
. Suppose that
q,t\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
are generic so that all non-symmetric Macdonald polynomials are well-defined, and (
242
) holds for
E_{j}
. Since it follows from (
48
) that the operator
\mathsf{B}
commutes with
\tau_{-}
,
E_{-j}
transforms in the same way as
E_{j}
under
\tau_{-}
. For
j<0
, we have

\displaystyle\tau_{-}(E_{1-j})=

\displaystyle t^{-1}q^{\frac{1}{2}}\pol(YXT)\cdot\tau_{-}(E_{j})

(243)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{induction}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle t^{-1}q^{\frac{1}{2}}q^{-\frac{j^{2}}{2}}t^{j}\pol(YXT)\cdot E_{j}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{AB-action}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle q^{\frac{1}{2}}q^{-\frac{j^{2}}{2}}t^{j}\pol(Y)\cdot E_{1-j}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Y-action}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle q^{-\frac{(1-j)^{2}}{2}}t^{-1+j}E_{1-j}~.

This completes the proof.

Furthermore, one can show from (
57
) that the raising (
85
) and lowering (
86
) operators behave under the
\tau_{-}
transformation as

\tau_{-}(\mathsf{R}_{j})=q^{-j-\frac{1}{2}}t^{-1}\mathsf{R}_{j}~,\qquad\tau_{-}(\mathsf{L}_{j})=q^{j-\frac{1}{2}}t\mathsf{L}_{j}~.

A similar induction shows that the symmetric Macdonald polynomials are transformed as

\tau_{-}(P_{j})=q^{-\frac{j^{2}}{2}}t^{-j}P_{j}~,

(244)

which is consistent with (
241
).
This formula indeed gives the modular
T
-transformation for representations of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
in §
2
.

B.2.2
Functional representation

Here we shall give an explicit definition of the functional representation of DAHA of rank one
(
Che 05
, §2.7.1)
. Restricted to the spherical DAHA
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
, it can be understood as the
S
-transformation
\sigma(\mathscr{P})
of the polynomial representation
\mathscr{P}
of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
as in §
2.5
and §
3.1
.

The assignment

f(X)~\mapsto~\textbf{[}f(X),E_{n}(X)\textbf{]}/E_{n}(t^{-1})=\begin{cases}f(q^{n}t)&\textrm{for}\ n>0\\
f(q^{n}t^{-1})&\textrm{for}\ n\leq 0\end{cases}

provides the function defined only over the set
\mathsf{Fun}
of
X=q^{n}t^{\textrm{sgn}(n-\frac{1}{2})}
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Thus, one can obtain the discretization of the polynomial representation (
235
) on this set of functions defined over
X\in\mathsf{Fun}
for
h\in\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}

\mathrm{disc}:\textbf{[}f(X),E_{n}(X)\textbf{]}/E_{n}(t^{-1})~\mapsto~\textbf{[}\pol(h)\cdot f(X),E_{n}(X)\textbf{]}/E_{n}(t^{-1})~.

More explicitly, one can read off the action as

\displaystyle(\mathrm{disc}(X)\cdot f)(q^{n}t^{\textrm{sgn}(n-\frac{1}{2})})

\displaystyle=q^{n}t^{\textrm{sgn}(n-\frac{1}{2})}f(q^{n}t^{\textrm{sgn}(n-\frac{1}{2})})~,

\displaystyle(\mathrm{disc}(\pi)\cdot f)(q^{n}t^{\textrm{sgn}(n-\frac{1}{2})})

\displaystyle=f(q^{1-n}t^{\textrm{sgn}(\frac{1}{2}-n)})~,

and

\displaystyle(\mathrm{disc}(T)\cdot f)(q^{n}t^{\textrm{sgn}(n-\frac{1}{2})})

\displaystyle=\frac{tq^{2n}t^{2\textrm{sgn}(n-\frac{1}{2})}-t^{-1}}{q^{2n}t^{2\textrm{sgn}(n-\frac{1}{2})}-1}f(q^{-n}t^{\textrm{sgn}(\frac{1}{2}-n)})-\frac{t-t^{-1}}{q^{2n}t^{2\textrm{sgn}(n-\frac{1}{2})}-1}f(q^{n}t^{\textrm{sgn}(n-\frac{1}{2})})~.

Though
q^{-n}t^{\textrm{sgn}(\frac{1}{2}-n)}
is not in the set
\mathsf{Fun}
when
n=0
,
tq^{2n}t^{2\textrm{sgn}(n-\frac{1}{2})}-t^{-1}=0
in this case. Therefore, it is well-defined.

The functional representation is defined by the symmetric bilinear form (
227
). If we restrict the functional representation to the spherical subalgebra
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}
, it is the modular
S
-transformation
\sigma
of the polynomial representation due to (
225
).

B.2.3
Trigonometric Cherednik algebra of type
A_{1}

To find the trigonometric degeneration, we set
X=e^{\beta\hat{x}},q=e^{\beta\hbar},t=e^{\beta\hbar c}
and
T=se^{\beta cs}
, where
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is the reflection. Then, taking the leading order in
\beta
from (
234
), the generators
s,\hat{x},Y
satisfy the following relations:

s^{2}=1~,\quad sY^{-1}s=Y~,\quad s\hat{x}+\hat{x}s=-2c~,\quad Y^{-1}\hat{x}Y-\hat{x}=\hbar+2cs~.

(245)

The algebra generated by
s,\hat{x},Y
with these relations is called the trigonometric Cherednik algebra
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{tri}}_{\hbar,c}
of type
A_{1}
. It enjoys a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-symmetry

\xi_{2}:s\mapsto s~,\ \hat{x}\mapsto\hat{x}~,\ Y\mapsto-Y~,\ \hbar\mapsto\hbar~,\ c\mapsto c~,

(246)

which is reminiscent of (
47
).
The idempotent is defined by

\mathbf{e}:=\frac{1+s}{2}~,

(247)

and the spherical subalgebra is defined as
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{tri}}_{\hbar,c}:=\mathbf{e}\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{tri}}_{\hbar,c}\mathbf{e}
, which is generated by

v:=\mathbf{e}\hat{x}^{2}\mathbf{e}~,\quad\textrm{and}\quad y=\mathbf{e}Y\mathbf{e}

(248)

B.2.4
Rational Cherednik algebra of type
A_{1}

In the trigonometric Cherednik algebra
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{tri}}_{\hbar,c}
, we write
Y=e^{\beta\hat{y}}
and we rescale
\hat{x}\mapsto\hat{x}/\beta
. Then, the generators
s,\hat{x},\hat{y}
satisfy the following relations

s^{2}=1~,\quad s\hat{x}=-\hat{x}s~,\quad s\hat{y}=-\hat{y}s~,\quad[\hat{x},\hat{y}]=\hbar+2cs~,

in the leading order of
\beta
.
The algebra generated by
s,\hat{x},\hat{y}
with these relations is the rational Cherednik algebra

\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{rat}}_{\hbar,c}
.

Let us study the spherical subalgebra
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{rat}}_{\hbar,c}:=\mathbf{e}\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{rat}}_{\hbar,c}\mathbf{e}
where the idempotent
\mathbf{e}
is the same as (
247
).
The spherical subalgebra
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{rat}}_{\hbar,c}
is a
\mathbb{C}[c,\hbar]
-algebra generated by

E=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{e}\hat{x}^{2}\mathbf{e}~,\qquad F=-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{e}\hat{y}^{2}\mathbf{e}~,\qquad H=-\mathbf{e}\hat{x}\hat{y}\mathbf{e}~.

with relations

\displaystyle[{E},{F}]=\hbar\Bigl({H}+\frac{\hbar}{2}+c\Bigr)~,

\displaystyle[{H},{E}]=2\hbar{E}~,\qquad[{H},{F}]=-2\hbar{F}~,

(249)

\displaystyle 4{E}{F}={H}(-{H}+\hbar-2c)~.

Thus,
E,F,H
can be interpreted as the
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
-triple.
Writing the Casimir element of
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
as

\Omega=EF+FE+\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(H+\frac{\hbar}{2}+c\Bigr)^{2}~,

it is easy to verify that there is an isomorphism
(
EG 02
, Proposition 8.2)

U(\mathfrak{sl}(2))/\langle\Omega-\tfrac{1}{2}(c+\tfrac{1}{2}\hbar)(c-\tfrac{3}{2}\hbar)\rangle\cong S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{\hbar,c}^{\textrm{rat}}~.

(250)

At
c=0
, the rational Cherednik algebra of type
A_{1}
is isomorphic to

\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{rat}}_{\hbar,c=1}=\mathscr{O}^{q}(\mathbb{C}^{2})\rtimes\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]~,

where
\hat{x},\hat{y}
are the generators of
\mathscr{O}^{q}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
and
s
is the generator of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. If we consider the open set
\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathbb{C}\subset\mathbb{C}^{2}
,
the deformation quantization
\mathscr{O}^{q}(\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathbb{C})
of its coordinate ring is generated by
\hat{x}^{\pm},\hat{y}
. Then, we can define a subalgebra generated by

\displaystyle E:=\frac{\hat{x}^{2}}{2}~,\qquad F:=-\frac{\hat{y}^{2}}{2}-c\hat{x}^{-1}\hat{y}~,\qquad H:=-\hat{x}\hat{y}~,

(251)

which is isomorphic to the spherical subalgebra
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{rat}}_{\hbar,c}
with generic
c
given in (
250
) with the relations (
249
). This construction indeed provides the polynomial representation (
232
) of the spherical subalgebra of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{rat}}_{\hbar,c}
acting on
\mathbb{C}[x^{\pm 2}]
by substituting
-\hbar\partial_{x}
for
\hat{y}
.

Appendix C
Quantum torus algebra

In this appendix, we study representations of quantum torus algebras and their symmetrization. We aim at understanding representations of the simplest quantum torus algebra in terms of 2d
A
-model on the affine variety
\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. Therefore, we first review representation theory of the quantum torus algebra, and we subsequently find
A
-branes for two important families of representations of the quantum torus algebra:
cyclic representations
and
polynomial representations
. The quantum torus algebra and DAHA are closely related, and the relationship becomes clearer when we symmetrize the algebra and representations by the distinguished outer automorphism
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Although representations of the quantum torus algebra and symmetrized quantum torus are rather simple, they play a helpful guide for analogous analysis for DAHA in §
2
.

C.1
Representations of quantum torus algebra

It is well-known that the so-called quantum torus algebra is given by

QT=\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm}]\langle X^{\pm},Y^{\pm}\rangle/(Y^{-1}X^{-1}YX=q^{-1})~.

(252)

Here the generator
q
is a formal parameter that commutes with both
X
and
Y
. This algebra occurs throughout physics, and it is indeed a close cousin of the most basic ingredient in the whole theory of quantum mechanics. If we allowed arbitrary (rather than integral) powers of
X
and
Y
, and set
q=\exp(2\pi i \hbar)
, it would be the group algebra of the exponentiated form of the canonical commutation relations.
Here, since only integral powers are allowed, we are dealing with a discrete-valued analogue of canonically conjugate variables.

In fact,
QT
is the group algebra of a specific Heisenberg group, associated to a discrete analogue of a symplectic pairing.
As is well-known, the linear representations of a discrete group correspond precisely to modules over its group algebra, so the presence of the scalars is irrelevant: studying
QT
-modules is the same as studying representations of this Heisenberg group.

To construct it, we introduce Heisenberg groups in somewhat greater generality. Let
S
be any locally compact topological ring with characteristic not equal to two, and
\mathsf{V}
a free, finitely generated
S
-module (so isomorphic to
S^{n}
). A symplectic pairing on
\mathsf{V}
is defined to be a function

\omega:\mathsf{V}\times\mathsf{V}\rightarrow S~,

(253)

which is skew-symmetric and
S
-bilinear. (Note that non-degeneracy condition on
\omega
is not necessary here so that we use a pairing rather than a symplectic form. However, in all the examples we consider below,
\omega
is a non-degenerate symplectic form.)
Then, the following is true:

Theorem C.1

(
(
Kle 65
, Theorem 7.1)
)
.

The group
H^{2}(\mathsf{V},S)
, which classifies group extensions of the additive group
\mathsf{V}
, is isomorphic to the group of symplectic pairings on
\mathsf{V}
.

(It is a pleasant exercise to check that any bilinear function defines an inhomogeneous group cochain. To obtain the full result, one shows that the antisymmetrization of any cochain is necessarily bilinear, and actually defines a set of unique representatives for the cohomology.)

There is therefore a unique central extension of groups defined by the sequence

0\rightarrow S\rightarrow\Heis(\mathsf{V},\omega)\rightarrow\mathsf{V}\rightarrow 0~,

(254)

which is called the Heisenberg group associated to the data
(\mathsf{V},\omega)
. In our example, we take
S
to be
\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathsf{V}
to be
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, and the pairing to be defined by sending a fixed ordered basis of
\mathsf{V}
to
+1
. It is then easy to see that
QT
is the group algebra of
\Heis(\mathbb{Z}^{2},\omega)
, and that we can similarly define the quantum torus algebra

QT(\mathsf{V},\omega)=\mathbb{C}[\Heis(\mathsf{V},\omega)]

(255)

associated to a general finitely generated free
\mathbb{Z}
-module with symplectic pairing. In the sequel,
QT
without decoration will always refer to the standard module
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with its standard pairing.

Our algebra
QT
has an interesting collection of outer automorphisms: in fact,

\Out(QT)=\Sp(2,\mathbb{Z})=\SL(2,\mathbb{Z}).

(256)

The inner automorphisms just consist of shifting
X
and
Y
by powers of
q
, and so make up the group
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. The full automorphism group is a central extension of
\SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
by this module:
