\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}~.

(257)

More generally, for the Heisenberg group on a higher-dimensional standard symplectic lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{2n}
, the group of automorphisms is the semidirect product
\mathbb{Z}^{2n}\rtimes\Sp(2n,\mathbb{Z})
.

Heisenberg groups over more general rings now make starring appearances in numerous branches of mathematics, including even number theory
(
Wei 64
, for example)
. For that reason (and to highlight their connection to DAHA), we have chosen to emphasize the generality of the concept here even though the cases of most interest to everyday physics are
S=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{Z}
. For some physical applications of the latter algebra in the context of Chern–Simons theory, see for example
Guk 05
and related work on the
AJ
-conjecture.

C.1.1
Unitary representations

The unitary representation theory of Heisenberg groups is well-understood, thanks to the Stone–von Neumann theorem and its generalizations due, in particular, to Mackey
Mac 49
;
Mac 76
. We will just quickly recall enough context to state the main theorem. (This subsection is intended to mathematically classify unitary representations of
QT
so that the reader could skip to the three cases at the end for the first reading.) Let us start quoting the following definition:

Definition C.2
.

Let
G
be a locally compact topological group and
\Gamma
a Borel space with
G
-action. A
system of imprimitivity based on
(G,\Gamma)
consists of a separable Hilbert space with a unitary, strongly continuous representation of
G
, together with
a
G
-equivariant, projection-valued measure, assigning projection operators on the Hilbert space to the Borel sets of
\Gamma
.

The basic example of such data is the space of
L^{2}
functions with respect to a
G
-invariant measure on
\Gamma
;
G
is then represented by pull-back, and the projections are just characteristic functions of subsets. Actually, one can show that essentially all systems of imprimitivity arise in this fashion.

The most interesting examples for physics arise when a group
\hat{G}
is the semidirect product of an abelian normal subgroup
A
with some other group
G
that acts on
A
: that is, there is a split short exact sequence

0\to A\to\hat{G}\to G\to 0~.

(258)

Then one chooses
\Gamma
to be the character space of the abelian normal subgroup
A
, with its obvious
G
-action.

Theorem C.3
.

The set of isomorphism classes of unitary irreducible representations of
\hat{G}
is in bijection with the set of systems of imprimitivity based on
(G,\Gamma)
, up to unitary equivalence.

It is further true
Var 68
that when
G
acts transitively on
\Gamma
, the inequivalent systems of imprimitivity based on
(G,\Gamma)
are in one-to-one correspondence with unitary representations of the point stabilizer subgroup

G_{x}=\{g\in G|g\cdot x=x\}\quad(x\in\Gamma)~.

(259)

This means that unitary representations of
\hat{G}
are classified by the
G
-orbits of
\Gamma
, together with unitary representations of the corresponding stabilizer subgroups
G_{x}
.

For the physically minded reader, this can be understood as generalizations of Wigner’s technique of the little group
Wig 39
. There, the decomposition (
258
) for the Poincaré group is the obvious (and canonical) one,

0\to\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}\rtimes\SO(d)\to\SO(d)\to 0~.

(260)

The theorem of Stone and von Neumann is indeed an example of precisely the same very powerful general logic.

To apply this technique to representations of
\Heis(\mathsf{V},\omega)
, we must choose a maximal isotropic subspace
\mathsf{L}
of
(\mathsf{V},\omega)
.
There is then a unique abelian normal subgroup
A_{\mathsf{L}}
of the Heisenberg group determined by
\mathsf{L}
, which can be non-canonically identified with
\mathsf{L}\oplus S
. In fact, this choice defines a (non-canonical) semidirect product structure

0\rightarrow A_{\mathsf{L}}\rightarrow\Heis(\mathsf{V},\omega)\rightarrow\mathsf{V}/\mathsf{L}\rightarrow 0.

(261)

Understanding representations thus reduces to studying the
(\mathsf{V}/\mathsf{L})
-orbits in
\Gamma
. For simplicity, let us reduce to the one-dimensional Heisenberg group, where
\mathsf{V}=S^{2}
and
\omega
is the standard Darboux pairing. Then
\Gamma=S^{\vee}\times S^{\vee}
is the product of two copies of the Pontryagin dual of
S
. If we abuse our exponential notation from (
252
) above, we can write an element of
\Heis(\mathsf{V},\omega)
as
q^{a}X^{b}Y^{c}
, where
a,b,c\in S
. Then,
A_{\mathsf{L}}
is the set of elements of the form
q^{a}X^{b}
, and a splitting of the short exact sequence is provided by the subgroup of elements
Y^{c}
. The action of
\mathsf{V}/\mathsf{L}
is then determined by the formula

Y^{-c}\Bigl(q^{a}X^{b}\Bigr)Y^{c}=q^{a-bc}X^{b}~,

(262)

which also specifies the action on
\Gamma
by pullback.

In our example of quantum torus algebra,
S=\mathbb{Z}
, so that
\Gamma=\U(1)\times\U(1)
. If we abuse notation, we can denote the image of
q
(the choice of central character) simply by
q
. Of course, Schur’s lemma ensures that the center must act by a chosen central character in any irreducible representation. This central character is nothing other than the numerical value of
\hbar
, and plays the same role in this more general context. As such, no confusion should arise, and we will frequently confuse formal and numerical
q
in what follows. We will denote the image of
X
by
x_{1}
. Then the group action on
\Gamma
is just

Y:(q,x_{1})\mapsto(q,x_{1}/q)~,

(263)

and of course fixes the central character.

It is straightforward to enumerate the orbits:

1.

q=1
. An orbit is any single point
x_{1}\in\U(1)
. The stabilizer is
\mathbb{Z}
. A representation is thus determined by
x_{1}
and another phase
y_{1}
. These are the abelian representations of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

2.

q\in\U(1)
is
not
a root of unity. There is a single orbit. The stabilizer is trivial. There is thus a unique irreducible representation up to isomorphism. (This is the Stone–von Neumann theorem.)

3.

q
is a primitive
m
-th root of unity
\mu_{m}
. The space of orbits is
\U(1)/\mu_{m}
, each orbit consisting of
m
points. The stabilizer is
m\mathbb{Z}\subset\mathbb{Z}
. A representation is again determined by two phases, one being the
m
-th power of
x_{1}
, the other being (in a precise sense) the “
m
-th power of
y_{1}
.”

In fact, we can construct the corresponding representations explicitly; we do this (in greater generality) in what follows.

C.1.2
Non-unitary representations

Let us first fix
q
to be a primitive
m
-th root of unity, corresponding to the third case above. There is then an obvious unitary finite-dimensional representation of the quantum torus algebra given by the well-known “clock” and “shift” matrices, acting on a standard vector space
\mathbb{C}^{m}
with basis
e_{i}
:

\xi e_{i}=e_{i+1},\quad\varpi^{-}e_{i}=q^{-i}e_{i}.

(264)

It is clear that mapping
X\mapsto\xi
and
Y\mapsto\varpi^{-}
defines the structure of an
m
-dimensional
QT
-module with central character
q
. What is perhaps slightly less obvious is that every such representation is closely related to this one, differing only by a rescaling.

Definition C.4

(cyclic representation)
.

Fix a pair
\lambda=(x_{m},y_{m})\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. Let
x_{1}
and
y_{1}
be any
m
-th roots of
x_{m}
and
y_{m}
respectively. Then the
cyclic representation of weight
\lambda
, denoted
\mathscr{F}^{\lambda}
, is defined on an
m
-dimensional complex vector space by the map

QT\rightarrow\End(\mathscr{F}^{\lambda}):X\mapsto x_{1}\xi,~Y\mapsto y_{1}\varpi^{-}.

(265)

(Note that the isomorphism type does not depend on the choices of
x_{1}
and
y_{1}
.) We may sometimes write
\mathscr{F}^{\lambda}_{m}
to specify the dimension of the representation.

For an
m
-th primitive root of unity
q
, it is known
Kas 94
;
Bai 07
;
Kim 19
that every representation of
QT
in which
X
and
Y
are invertible is a direct sum of irreducible representations of the form
\mathscr{F}^{\lambda}
for some choices of weights. Two representations
\mathscr{F}^{\lambda}
and
\mathscr{F}^{\lambda^{\prime}}
are isomorphic precisely when
\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}
.
Note that the weight
\lambda
and
q
together define a central character in this instance. Any representation must factor through the quotient
QT/(q^{m}-1)
, in which the elements
X^{m}
and
Y^{m}
are also central.

If
q
is generic, we have no hope of finding a finite-dimensional representation by the results of the previous subsection, but the construction above generalizes to produce an infinite-dimensional module. Let
\mathscr{P}=\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm},X^{\pm}]
be the space of Laurent polynomials in the central generator
q
and the variable
X
.
We can define an analogue of the operator
\varpi^{-}
above by the rule

\varpi^{-}X^{i}=q^{-i}X^{i}.

(266)

The role of the matrix
\xi
will be played simply by multiplication by
X
.

Definition C.5

(polynomial representation)
.

Choose a weight
y_{1}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. The
polynomial representation of weight
y_{1}
, denoted
\mathscr{P}^{y_{1}}
, is defined by the map

QT\to\End(\mathscr{P}^{y_{1}}):X\mapsto X,~Y\mapsto y_{1}\varpi^{-}.

(267)

It is clear that the shift automorphism (multiplication by
X
) intertwines the representations
\mathscr{P}^{y_{1}}
and
\mathscr{P}^{y_{1}/q}
. However, this automorphism is outer (it is not
conjugation
by
X
, but rather a generator of
\SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
), so that these representations are not actually isomorphic.

C.1.3
Geometric viewpoint

To obtain a geometric perspective on its representation theory, we will take a slightly different way of looking at the algebra
QT
. If we set
q=1
(or, equivalently, taking the quotient of
QT
by
\mathbb{C}[S]
), we obtain a commutative group algebra. We are free to think of any commutative algebra as the coordinate ring of a certain affine space. For our example, it is indeed the product of two punctured affine lines:

QT\xrightarrow[q\to 1]{}\mathscr{O}(\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}).

(268)

This space will play an important role in the sequel, and so we will denote it by
\mathfrak{X}
. In this paper, we consider the representation theory of more complicated algebras, and
\mathfrak{X}
will therefore denote more complicated spaces, depending on the context. (As our main interest,
\mathfrak{X}
in §
2
is the moduli space of flat
\SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-connections on the once-punctured torus.)

What is common between all of these examples are certain key properties of
\mathfrak{X}
: First of all, it will always be a non-compact complex manifold, so that it has a large and interesting algebra
\mathscr{O}(\mathfrak{X})
of holomorphic functions with polynomial growth at infinity. (In fact, in this paper,
\mathfrak{X}
will always be an affine variety over
\mathbb{C}
.)
It will also be a holomorphic symplectic manifold; in our current example, there is a natural holomorphic symplectic form on
\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, which we take to be

\Omega_{J}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{dX}{X}\wedge\frac{dY}{Y}.

(269)

In our examples,
\mathfrak{X}
will be even hyper-Kähler ; for reasons that will become clear later, we refer to the complex structure whose holomorphic symplectic structure is of interest to us now as
J
, motivating the choice of notation above. In the example at hand, we can see the hyper-Kähler structure explicitly by using logarithmic coordinates,

X=\exp(r + i \varphi)~,\qquad Y=\exp(\rho+ i \phi)~,

(270)

and observing that both the real and imaginary parts of

\Omega_{J}=\omega_{K}+i\omega_{I}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\Bigl(dr\wedge d\phi+d\varphi\wedge d\rho\Bigr)+\frac{i}{2\pi}\Bigl(d\varphi\wedge d\phi-dr\wedge d\rho\Bigr)

(271)

are real symplectic forms. The third such symplectic structure is

\omega_{J}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\Bigl(dr\wedge d\varphi+d\rho\wedge d\phi\Bigr);

(272)

it arises by identifying our space with the cotangent bundle of the two-torus.

Of course, it is also trivial to see that this space admits an elliptic fibration, which can be written very simply in coordinates as

\displaystyle\pi:\mathfrak{X}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{C},

(273)

\displaystyle(X,Y)

\displaystyle\mapsto(r,\rho)~.

It is apparent that this map is holomorphic in complex structure
I
, and that its fibers are tori which are Lagrangian with respect to
\omega_{J}
and
\omega_{K}
. As such, in the language of mirror symmetry for hyper-Kähler manifolds
KW 07
;
GW 08
, they are appropriate submanifolds to support branes of type
(B,A,A)
, which are the only topologically interesting compact Lagrangian branes in the
A
-model of
(\mathfrak{X},\omega_{K})
.

In addition,
QT
is actually the deformation quantization
\mathscr{O}^{q}(\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times})
of the coordinate ring with respect to the Poisson bracket defined by
\Omega_{J}
. We can thus begin to think about the algebra
QT
within the context of brane quantization
GW 09
in 2d sigma-model, which is reviewed in §
2.3
and §
2.4
. It is the central idea of this paper, and this rather simple setting in this Appendix is instructive guidance for a similar geometric angle on DAHA in §
2
. (See also
Soi 01
for the geometric approach to the quantum tori.)

C.2
Branes for quantum torus algebra

We have understood the representation theory of
QT
. Also, we recall that the curvature
F
of the
\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}}
line bundle in 2d sigma-model on the symplectic manifold
(\mathfrak{X}=\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times},\omega_{\mathfrak{X}})
is subject to

F+B+i\omega_{\mathfrak{X}}=\frac{\Omega_{J}}{i\hbar}~,

to obtain
QT
as
\Hom(\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}},\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}})
. Now, we set up an equivalence between the categories
\Rep(QT)
and
\text{$A$-}{\Brane}(\mathfrak{X},\omega_{\mathfrak{X}})
by finding
A
-branes for the cyclic representations (
265
) and the polynomial representations
267
.
It is the essential motivation for the study of our main examples, DAHA, but this builds intuition for the sorts of matching in §
2.5
and §
2.6.1
.

C.2.1
Cyclic representations

Our first attempt will be to find
A
-branes for the finite-dimensional representations (
265
). On general grounds, these should arise from branes supported on compact Lagrangians.
These branes are special because, from the geometry viewpoint, compact Lagrangian submanifolds are interesting and somewhat rare. Similarly, finite-dimensional representations are rare and distinctive from the standpoint of representation theory.

Due to the simple topology of
\mathfrak{X}\simeq T^{*}T^{2}
in this example, there is essentially one interesting class of compact branes: Lagrangian tori wrapping the unique generator of
H_{2}(\mathfrak{X},\mathbb{Z})
. The condition that Lagrangians have vanishing Maslov class requires that no cycle in
H_{1}(\mathbf{L},\mathbb{Z})
bound a disc in
\mathfrak{X}
; as such, it must map injectively to
H_{1}(\mathfrak{X},\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
under the obvious map.

These tori are holomorphic in complex structure
I
and holomorphic Lagrangians with respect to
\Omega_{I}
so that they can be Lagrangian submanifolds in a symplectic manifold
(\mathfrak{X},\omega_{\mathfrak{X}})
only when
\omega_{\mathfrak{X}}
is proportional to
\omega_{K}
. According to (
64
), we need to set
\theta=0
, and the parameter
\hbar
is real.
It is easy to see that each equivalence class of such tori under Hamiltonian isotopies with respect to
\omega_{K}
contains precisely one torus of the form
\pi^{-1}(r,\rho)
,
i.e.
a fiber of the map (
273
). It is furthermore clear that no two such tori are equivalent. Each
A
-brane thus corresponds to exactly one special Lagrangian brane of type
(B,A,A)
, and branes are indexed by two real parameters
(r,\rho)
recording their position. (See Figure
21
.)

Now, as we recalled above, an
A
-brane is a Lagrangian equipped with (among other data) a flat unitary line bundle. To choose such a flat line bundle on the torus, we need to additionally choose two
\U(1)
holonomies, or equivalently pick a point in
\Jac(T^{2})\cong(T^{2})^{\vee}
. Thus, we write the two
\U(1)
holonomies by
(e^{i\varphi^{\vee}},e^{i\phi^{\vee}})
.
The set of
A
-branes is thus labeled by two real for the position and two circle-valued parameters for the holonomies. This agrees precisely with the set
\lambda=(x_{m},y_{m})
of the cyclic representations
\mathscr{F}^{\lambda}
of
QT
in (
265
) where the identification is

(x_{m},y_{m})=(e^{r+i\varphi^{\vee}},e^{\rho+i\phi^{\vee}})~.

(274)

The last thing to check is the dimension of the space of open strings
\Hom(\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}},\mathfrak{B}_{\mathbf{L}})
. Since
\theta=0
in (
64
), the data of
\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}}
consist of
F+B=\omega_{I}/|\hbar|
and
\omega_{\mathfrak{X}}=-\omega_{K}/|\hbar|
. Also, the Chan-Paton bundle for the Lagrangian brane is endowed with flat connection (
75
). As a result, the dimension formula (
78
) becomes

\dim\Hom(\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}},\mathfrak{B}_{\mathbf{L}})=\int_{T^{2}}\frac{\omega_{I}}{2\pi\hbar}=\frac{1}{\hbar}~.

(275)

Since this is the dimension of holomorphic sections,
\hbar=1/m
for some positive integer
m
. We can interpret this as the Bohr–Sommerfeld quantizability condition on the compact Lagrangian branes, and we therefore recover the condition that
q
is a primitive
m
-th root of unity for the cyclic representation.

The involution

\zeta:\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{X};~(r,\varphi,\rho,\phi)\mapsto(-r,\varphi,-\rho,\phi)

is holomorphic in complex structure
I
and antiholomorphic in complex structures
J
and
K
. The central Lagrangian torus
\pi^{-1}(0,0)
is exactly the set of fixed points under
\zeta
, and the
A
-brane on
\pi^{-1}(0,0)
with the trivial holonomies gives rise to the unitary finite-dimensional representation, which is the third case at the end of Appendix
C.1.1
.

Figure 21:
A brane (red) supported on a torus fiber gives the cyclic representation and the deformation parameter consists of its position
(r,\rho)
and the
\U(1)^{2}
holonomy. A brane (blue) supported on
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})_{X}
provides the polynomial representation and the deformation parameter consists of its position
|y_{1}|
and the
\U(1)
holonomy.

C.2.2
Polynomial representations

Next, we will find an
A
-brane corresponding to the polynomial representation
\mathscr{P}^{y_{1}}
in (
267
). Since it is an infinite-dimensional representation, we expect this brane to be non-compact.

Let us begin by just considering Lagrangian subspaces which are isomorphic to
T^{*}S^{1}
, embedded such that the generator of
H_{1}
is mapped to the generator of
H^{1}((\mathbb{C}^{\times})_{X})
under the embedding map.
While this choice is not canonical, other classes in
H_{1}(\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times})
could be obtained by acting with the outer automorphism group
\SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
.
We are thus interested in Lagrangians that are graphs
\Gamma_{Y}
of topologically trivial maps
Y=(\rho,\phi):(\mathbb{C}^{\times})_{X}\to(\mathbb{C}^{\times})_{Y}
, which can be represented in the form

\Gamma_{Y}:=\{(X,Y(X))=(r,\varphi,\rho(r,\varphi),\phi(r,\varphi))\subset(\mathbb{C}^{\times})_{X}\times(\mathbb{C}^{\times})_{Y}\}~~.

(276)

Recall that the symplectic form (
64
) we are using is

\omega_{\mathfrak{X}}=-\frac{\sin\theta}{2\pi|\hbar|}(d\varphi\wedge d\phi-dr\wedge d\rho)-\frac{\cos\theta}{2\pi|\hbar|}(dr\wedge d\phi+d\varphi\wedge d\rho)~,

(277)

The condition for (
276
) to define a Lagrangian submanifold is therefore

\sin\theta\Bigl(\partialderivative{\phi}{r}+\partialderivative{\rho}{\varphi}\Bigr)+\cos\theta\Bigl(\partialderivative{\rho}{r}-\partialderivative{\phi}{\varphi}\Bigr)=0~.

(278)

The two quantities in parentheses are just the two Cauchy–Riemann equations for the function
Y
. Therefore, the graph of a holomorphic map
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})_{X}
to
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})_{Y}
defines an
A
-brane for any choice of
\omega_{\mathfrak{X}}
—in fact, an
(A,B,A)
-brane. But the only such holomorphic function that is isotopic to the constant map is itself the constant map!

Let us recall that we consider
A
-branes up to Hamiltonian isotopy. The Hamiltonian vector field associated to a generating function
f(r,\varphi)
is

X_{f}=2\pi|\hbar|\Bigl[\cos\theta\Bigl(\partialderivative{f}{r}\partialderivative{\phi}+\partialderivative{f}{\varphi}\partialderivative{\rho}\Bigr)-\sin\theta\Bigl(\partialderivative{f}{r}\partialderivative{\rho}-\partialderivative{f}{\varphi}\partialderivative{\phi}\Bigr)\Bigr],

(279)

which generate the Hamiltonian flow of the form

\delta\phi=\cos\theta\partialderivative{f}{r}+\sin\theta\partialderivative{f}{\varphi},\qquad\delta\rho=\cos\theta\partialderivative{f}{\varphi}-\sin\theta\partialderivative{f}{r}~.

(280)

It is easiest to understand the action of these vector fields if
\theta=0
. Choosing
f(r,\varphi)=r
then generates a global rotation of
\phi
in
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})_{Y}
, constant over the Lagrangian, and any radially-dependent rotation can also be obtained by a suitable choice of
f
. As such, the angle
\phi
can be set to any chosen value anywhere on the Lagrangian; angular displacements do not lead to different objects in the
A
-model of
(\mathfrak{X},\omega_{K})
! The other Hamiltonian vector field ensures that we can deform our Lagrangian by a Hamiltonian isotopy to set
\rho(r,\varphi)
to its average value over
\varphi
, depending (in principle) on
r
. But the Lagrangian condition (
278
) then ensures that
\rho
is just constant.

As such, every
A
-brane in
(\mathfrak{X},\omega_{K})
of topological class
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
can be brought by Hamiltonian isotopy to a unique
(A,B,A)
-brane which is a fiber of the projection on the second factor of
\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.
This makes it easy to see that the Maslov class is zero; we can consider the symplectic universal cover of
\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}
by
\mathbb{C}^{2}
, and such Lagrangians descend from affine subspaces there (which always have zero Maslov class).

There are exactly two real parameters defining an
(A,B,A)
-brane of this form in the category
\text{$A$-}{\Brane}(\mathfrak{X},\omega_{\mathfrak{X}})
: the modulus
|Y|
(or
\rho
) and the
\U(1)
holonomy of the flat bundle over the Lagrangian due to
H_{1}(\mathbf{L},\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}
.
This matches precisely with the
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
parameter
y_{1}
for the polynomial representation
\mathscr{P}^{y_{1}}
. Indeed, the representation space was just the algebra
\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm},X^{\pm}]
of Laurent polynomials, which is exactly the collection of holomorphic functions (in complex structure
J
) on this brane.

The story for generic values of
\theta
is similar. However, the integral curves of the Hamiltonian vector field generated by
\partial f/\partial r
are no longer closed circles, but rather spirals in
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
(obtained as the images of lines of fixed slope under the exponential map).

C.3
Symmetrized quantum torus

Now let us move one step forward to DAHA.
Recall from (
256
) that the outer automorphism group of
QT
is
\SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
. How much of this symmetry is visible in the classical limit?
Since the classical limit corresponds to a trivial choice of the central character (or, equivalently, to the quotient by the ideal
(q-1)
), all inner automorphisms must act trivially, so that the residual symmetry is by the group
\Out(QT)=\SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
. Indeed, this is just the natural action of the group of canonical transformations on symplectic
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
Of course, these automorphisms act on
\mathfrak{X}
by the exponentiated form of this action:

\SL(2,\mathbb{Z})\ni\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}:(X,Y)\mapsto\Bigl(X^{a}Y^{b},X^{c}Y^{d}\Bigr)~.

(281)

We will be particularly interested in one distinguished outer automorphism of order two, namely the central element
\kappa
of
\SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
.
Let us study the extension of
QT
by this outer automorphism. In other words, we are interested in the algebra
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{t=1}
defined by the short exact sequence

0\to QT\to\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{t=1}\to\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]\to 0~,

(282)

with respect to the outer automorphism
\kappa
. (Of course, this is just obtained by applying the group algebra functor to a corresponding semidirect product of groups.) We can think of this as adjoining a new generator
T
of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
to
QT
, subject to the relations

T^{2}=1~,\quad TXT=X^{-1}~,\quad TY^{-1}T=Y~.

(283)

In the classical limit,
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{t=1}
is not commutative. We therefore have no hope of telling a story analogous to the one we have been building for
QT
. However, rather than studying
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{t=1}
—which is like a
\kappa
-
equivariant
version of
QT
—directly, we can imagine trying to replace it by a
\kappa
-
invariant
version, whose classical limit should be (the functions on) the quotient of
\mathfrak{X}
by
\kappa
.

This is concretely accomplished as follows. The algebra
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{t=1}
contains an idempotent element
\mathbf{e}=(1+T)/2
, which one can think of as implementing projection onto
\kappa
-invariants. We can therefore define the subalgebra

S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{t=1}=\mathbf{e}\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{t=1}\mathbf{e}~.

(284)

In fact, the algebras
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{t=1}
and
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{t=1}
are Morita equivalent; this equivalence is witnessed by the bimodule
\mathbf{e}\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{t=1}

Obl04a
;
Obl04b
, and it is also discussed in §
4.3
.
We are thus free to study the representation category of either, and in the classical limit, we have that

S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{t=1}\xrightarrow[q\to 1]{}\mathscr{O}(\mathfrak{X})^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}=\mathscr{O}[(\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times})/\mathbb{Z}_{2}]~.

(285)

However, there is one subtlety. Since the geometric ramification parameter
\tilde{t}
and DAHA parameter
t
are related in (
56
),
t=1
means
\tilde{t}=q^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. Therefore,
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{t=1}
can be understood as the deformation quantization of the coordinate ring of
\mathcal{M}_{\text{flat}}(C_{p},\SL(2,\mathbb{C}))
with ramification

{}_{p}+i\talpha_{p}=i\hbar~.

(286)

Or, we believe that this
\frac{1}{2}
shift is related to the
orbifold (Gepner) point

(\talpha_{p},\tbeta_{p},{}_{p},\teta_{p})=(0,0,0,\frac{1}{2})

Asp 95
;
Dou 97
;
BI 97
;
Wen 02
in the sigma-model on
(\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times})/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. In fact, the evaluation of the left-hand side of (
67
) at the orbifold point yields
\frac{1}{2}
. If
(\omega_{\mathfrak{X}}^{-1}(F+B))^{2}=-1
is satisfied, the canonical coisotropic brane
\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}}
can exist at the orbifold point. However,
\mathfrak{B}_{\text{cc}}
at the orbifold point is obscure because of the stringy nature so that we leave it for future work. Also, it is worth noting that the skein algebra
\Sk(T^{2})
of a torus discussed in §
2.5
is isomorphic to
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{t=1}
. Hence,
\Sk(T^{2})
is
not
simply the deformation quantization of the coordinate ring of
\mathcal{M}_{\text{flat}}(T^{2},\SL(2,\mathbb{C}))
. This stems from the fact that a Higgs bundle over a genus one curve
C\cong T^{2}
is
not
stable. Thus, we need to take into account the quantum correction described above.

Now let us consider representation theory of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{t=1}
.
How can we study representations of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{t=1}
using the information we have from the non-equivariant case? In particular, can we make use of our understanding of
QT
-modules?
Our situation is summarized by the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{t=1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{t=1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces QT}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\End(U)\qquad.}

(287)

The astute reader will notice that there seems to be an obvious arrow missing in (
287
) above.
Isn’t
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{t=1}
simply the invariant subset of
QT
with respect to the action of
\kappa
? The answer is yes, but
not canonically
. Indeed, an outer automorphism is an
equivalence class
of automorphisms, and we must choose a particular
lift to a specific non-inner automorphism in order to even talk about the action of
\kappa
on
QT
. For example, we could take

\hat{\kappa}:X\mapsto X^{-1},~Y\mapsto Y^{-1}~,

(288)

but any other choice that shifts
X
and
Y
by powers of
q
is equally valid and does not change the structure of the extension
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{t=1}
.

Let us fix the lift (
288
).
It is then clear that the data of a lift of a
QT
-module
U
to an
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{t=1}
-module consists of an order-two endomorphism
\tau
of
U

such that conjugation by
\tau

and intertwining with any lift of the outer automorphism
\kappa
to an automorphism of
QT
. That is, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces QT}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\End(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces QT}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\End(U)~,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\kappa}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}

(289)

should commute, where the action of
\tau
on
\End(U)
is by conjugation. We can therefore lift the classification of
QT
-modules to
\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{t=1}
by classifying such lifts, and we will do this in the next subsection.

C.3.1
Representation theory

In this subsection, we consider each
QT
-module in turn. A
lifting datum
for a module
U
consists, as above, of an order-two endomorphism
\tau
of
U
that intertwines with
\hat{\kappa}
.

Proposition C.6
.

Lifting data for the polynomial representation
\mathscr{P}^{y_{1}}
exist precisely when
y_{1}^{2}=q^{\ell}
for some integer
\ell
. When this condition holds, there is a unique lift up to a sign.

Proof.

Let us consider the matrix elements of
\tau
in the polynomial basis:

\tau(X^{i})=\tau_{ij}X^{j}~.

(290)

We first consider the requirement imposed by conjugating with
X
:

\tau X=X^{-1}\tau~\Rightarrow\tau_{(i+1)j}=\tau_{i(j+1)}~.

(291)

This means that we can write our map in the form

\tau:X^{i}\mapsto\sum_{\ell}a_{\ell}X^{\ell-i}

(292)

for some constants
a_{\ell}
. It remains to consider the action on
Y
:

\displaystyle\tau Y=Y^{-1}\tau

\displaystyle\Rightarrow y_{1}q^{-i}a_{\ell}X^{\ell-i}=a_{\ell}y_{1}^{-1}q^{\ell-i}X^{\ell-i}

(293)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow a_{\ell}(y_{1}^{2}-q^{\ell})=0~.

From here it follows that
a_{\ell}=0
unless
\ell
is chosen such that
y_{1}^{2}=q^{\ell}
. In order to get an involution, we must choose
a_{\ell}=\pm 1
. Since
q
is not a root of unity, this completes the proof.
∎

Note that the freedom in the choice of
\ell
is not very interesting: it is always possible to shift
y_{1}\mapsto y_{1}/q
by a (non-inner) automorphism of the algebra. Hence, setting
\ell=0
, we may consider
\tau(X)=X^{-1}
, and the polynomial representation can be lifted when
y_{1}=\pm 1
.

Proposition C.7
.

Let
q
be a primitive
m
-th root of unity.
Lifting data for the cyclic representation
\mathscr{F}^{\lambda}_{m}
exist precisely when both weights are equal to plus or minus one. As above, there is then a unique lift up to a sign for each integer
\ell\pmod{m}
.

Proof.

The calculation proceeds along similar lines. By conjugating with
X
, we obtain the requirement

x_{1}\tau_{(i+1)j}=x_{1}^{-1}\tau_{i(j+1)}~.

(294)

Since the indices are cyclic, this requirement can only be satisfied if
x_{1}^{2m}=1
,
i.e.
if the weight
x_{m}=\pm 1
. The calculation for
Y
is identical, and produces the restriction

a_{\ell}(y_{1}^{2}-q^{\ell})=0~.

(295)

Since
q
is an
m
-th root of unity, this implies that
y_{m}=\pm 1
.
∎

This completes the story as to lifting of irreducible representations of
QT
. However, it is important to note that
reducible
representations can admit interesting new choices of lifting datum!

Proposition C.8
.

Lifting data for the representation
\mathscr{P}^{y_{1}}\oplus\mathscr{P}^{y_{1}^{\prime}}
exist whenever
y_{1}=q^{\ell}/y_{1}^{\prime}
for some
\ell
. When this is true, there is a unique lift up to sign. If either
y_{1}
or
y_{1}^{\prime}
satisfies the conditions of Proposition
C.6
, additional lifts correspondingly exist.

Proof.

This proceeds by computation, as above.
\tau
can be decomposed into four blocks, each of which must satisfy (
291
) independently; correspondingly, there are four undetermined constants, which we will call
a_{\ell}
,
b_{\ell}
,
c_{\ell}
, and
d_{\ell}
. The conjugation by
Y
then produces the conditions

a_{\ell}(y_{1}^{2}-q^{\ell})=d_{\ell}({y_{1}^{\prime}}^{2}-q^{\ell})=0~,\quad b_{\ell}(y_{1}y_{1}^{\prime}-q^{\ell})=c_{\ell}(y_{1}y_{1}^{\prime}-q^{\ell})=0~.

(296)

The conditions are decoupled and those on
a
and
d
just recover the conditions of Proposition
C.6
. The new conditions will also be satisfied for at most one choice of
\ell
, and we must choose
b_{\ell}c_{\ell}=1
to obtain an involution.
∎

Proposition C.9
.

Let
q
be a primitive
m
-th root of unity. Lifting data for the representation
\mathscr{F}^{\lambda}_{m}\oplus\mathscr{F}_{m}^{\lambda^{\prime}}
consist whenever
x_{m}x_{m}^{\prime}=1
and
y_{m}y_{m}^{\prime}=1
. When this is true, there is a unique lift up to sign. If either
y_{1}
or
y_{1}^{\prime}
satisfies the conditions of Proposition
C.6
, additional lifts correspondingly exist.

Proof.

As above, the diagonal blocks reduce independently to the previous case (Proposition
C.7
). The conjugation by
X
leads to the condition

x_{1}\tau_{(i+1)\bar{j}}=x_{1}^{-1}\tau_{i(\bar{j}+1)}

(297)

on the off-diagonal block; from cyclicity it then follows that
x_{1}x_{1}^{\prime}
is an
m
-th root of unity, and therefore that
x_{m}x_{m}^{\prime}=1
. The calculation for
Y
is identical, but reduces as in the proof of Proposition
C.7
since
q
is an
m
-th root of unity.
∎

After choosing
\hat{\kappa}
, the algebra
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{t=1}
maps injectively into
QT
, and we can therefore pull back representations. Consequently, the polynomial representation, the lift of
\mathscr{P}^{y_{1}}\oplus\mathscr{P}^{y_{1}^{\prime}}
with
y_{1}y_{1}^{\prime}=1
, generically becomes an
irreducible
representation of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{t=1}
on Laurent polynomials which are
not
symmetrized. However, when
y_{1}=\pm 1
, (or, to be precise,
y_{1}^{2}=q^{\ell}
for
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
), the spectrum of the
Y
-operator on
\mathscr{P}^{\pm 1}
becomes two-fold degenerate under
Y\mapsto Y^{-1}
. Thus,
\mathscr{P}^{\pm 1}
is compatible with the outer automorphism
\hat{\kappa}
. Correspondingly, the representation
\mathscr{P}^{\pm 1}
splits into two isomorphic irreducible representations on symmetric and antisymmetric Laurent polynomials.

For finite-dimensional representations, the story is identical. The pullback of the lift of
\mathscr{F}^{\lambda}_{m}\oplus\mathscr{F}_{m}^{\lambda^{\prime}}
to
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{t=1}
is generically irreducible, but becomes reducible when
m=2n
is even and
y_{m}=\pm 1
. Precisely in this case, it becomes an
n
-dimensional representation of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{t=1}
on
symmetric
Laurent polynomials, modulo the ideal
(X^{n}+X^{-n})
.

C.3.2
Corresponding branes

While we need to take into account the quantum correction (
286
),
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{t=1}
is related to the coordinate ring on
(\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times})/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
as in (
285
). Therefore, let us briefly consider the interpretation in terms of the
A
-brane category on
(\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times})/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

First, we consider
A
-branes corresponding to the polynomial representations. As in Appendix
C.2.2
, an
A
-brane for the polynomial representation of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{t=1}
is supported on the constant locus of the symmetrized function
Y+Y^{-1}
, and it stays an
A
-brane at any value of
\hbar
since it is of type
(A,B,A)
. Its preimages to
\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}
are generically two distinct branes supported on the
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})_{X}
planes with the values of
Y
related by
y_{1}y_{1}^{\prime}=1
. This corresponds to the representation of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{t=1}
on
non-symmetric
Laurent polynomials corresponding to Proposition
C.6
, and it has the deformation parameter valued in
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
for positions and the
\U(1)
holonomy. However, at a fixed point of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
action, only one brane for
\mathscr{P}^{\pm 1}
can be symmetrized into a brane on
\mathbb{C}^{\times}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, and it therefore gives rise to the representation on
symmetric
Laurent polynomials as in Proposition
C.6
.
The extensions of these representations to DAHA are discussed in §
2.5
.

Since the target
(\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times})/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is the moduli space of flat
\SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-connections over a torus
T^{2}
, it admits an elliptic fibration called the
Hitchin fibration
, which was discussed in §
2.1
. Roughly speaking, the elliptic fibration can be obtained by identifying the elliptic fibers in (
273
) at
(r,\rho)
and
(-r,-\rho)
so that there is a singular fiber
T^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
at
(r,\rho)=(0,0)
. Analogous to Appendix
C.2.1
, an
A
-brane supported on a generic fiber in the Hitchin fibration gives rise to an
m
-dimensional representation when
\hbar=1/m
for
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. Since its preimage in
\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}
under the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
quotient consists of two distinct tori at different positions, it is precisely the pullback of the lift of
\mathscr{F}_{m}^{\lambda}\oplus\mathscr{F}_{m}^{\lambda^{-1}}
in Proposition
C.9
. Consequently, there is the deformation parameter
\lambda
for a position at the base and the
\U(1)\times\U(1)
holonomy of the
A
-brane on a generic fiber.
The fiber at the center
(r,\rho)=(0,0)
in (
273
) is fixed as a set under the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
action so that the singular fiber in the Hitchin fibration is doubly covered by the torus. Thus, if the representation
\mathscr{F}^{(\pm 1,\pm 1)}_{m}
is even-dimensional
(m=2n)
, the corresponding
A
-brane degenerates two copies on the singular fiber. The
A
-brane with support on the singular fiber brings about the
n
-dimensional irreducible representation of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{t=1}
on the symmetric Laurent polynomials, obtained by the pullback of the lifting
\mathscr{F}_{2n}^{(\pm 1,\pm 1)}
.
There are no longer any deformation parameters, as this brane does not belong to any continuous family of
A
-branes. As explained in §
2.4
, a Chan-Paton bundle is generally endowed with a flat
\Spin^{c}
structure and the choice of signs corresponds to that of a flat
\Spin^{c}
structure with no holonomy (equivalently Spin structure in this case) of
K^{-1/2}_{\mathbf{F}}
. Indeed, the plus sign
+
is the Ramond spin structure on a circle (a trivial real line bundle over a circle), and the minus sign
-
is the Neveu-Schwarz spin structure (see §
2.6.2
and §
2.7
).

Although we see similar phenomena in branes for DAHA, finite-dimensional representations of DAHA are much richer because compact Lagrangians in the target for DAHA are more intricate, which is the main subject in §
2
.

Appendix D
3d
\mathcal{N}=4
theories and Cherednik algebras

In this appendix, we shall briefly discuss the relationship between Coulomb branches of 3d
\mathcal{N}=4
theories and the trigonometric and rational degeneration of DAHA of type
A_{1}
. The connection of the deformation quantization of Coulomb branches of 3d
\mathcal{N}=4
theories to variants of Cherednik algebras has been studied in
BFM 05
;
OY 16
;
BFN 18
;
KN 18
. Here we shall provide a brief review as well as some implications to representation theory from brane quantization.

D.1
Coulomb branches of 3d
\mathcal{N}=4
theories

If a 3d
\mathcal{N}=4
theory admits Lagrangian description, a Lagrangian is described by a 3d
\mathcal{N}=4
vector multiplet
\mathcal{V}
and a 3d
\mathcal{N}=4
hypermultiplet
\mathcal{H}
of gauge group
G
in a representation
R
of gauge group
G
. Moreover, they can be constructed a 3d
\mathcal{N}=2
vector multiplet
(A_{\mu},\sigma,\lambda_{\alpha},d)
and a 3d
\mathcal{N}=2
chiral multiplet
(\varphi,\psi_{\alpha},F)
where
\phi
is a complex scalar, and
\sigma
is a real scalar which can be regarded as the reminiscent of the
A_{3}
component of 4d gauge field. In fact, the
\mathcal{N}=4
vector multiplet consists of a 3d
\mathcal{N}=2
vector multiplet
(A_{\mu},\sigma,\lambda_{\alpha},d)
and a 3d
\mathcal{N}=2
chiral multiplet
(\varphi,\psi_{\alpha},F)
in the adjoint representation, forming

\mathcal{V}=(A_{\mu},\Lambda_{\alpha a\dot{a}},\Phi_{ab},D_{\dot{a}\dot{b}})~.

The gauge field
A_{\mu}
, gaugino
\Lambda_{\alpha a\dot{a}}
, scalar
\Phi_{ab}
and auxiliary field
D_{\dot{a}\dot{b}}
transform in the trivial,
(\mathbf{2},\mathbf{2})
,
(\mathbf{3},\mathbf{1})
, and
(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{3})
, respectively, under the
\SU(2)_{C}\times\SU(2)_{H}

R
-symmetry of a 3d
\mathcal{N}=4
theory.
The
\mathcal{N}=4
hypermultiplet consists of
\mathcal{N}=2
chiral multiplets in the representation
R
and its conjugate representation
\overline{R}
, forming

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}=(q_{\dot{a}},\widetilde{q}_{\dot{a}},\psi_{\alpha a},\widetilde{\psi}_{\alpha a})

(298)

The scalars
q_{\dot{a}},\widetilde{q}_{\dot{a}}
and their fermionic superpartners
\psi_{\alpha a},\widetilde{\psi}_{\alpha a}
transform as
(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{2})
and
(\mathbf{2},\mathbf{1})
, respectively, under the
R
-symmetry.

An
\mathcal{N}=4
gauge theory generically has a union of moduli spaces of vacua, called Coulomb branch and Higgs branch. The Higgs branch is a hyper-Kähler manifold, known as a Nakajima quiver variety
Nak 94
, parameterized by the expectation values of gauge-invariant operators in hypermultiplets.
The Coulomb branch
\mathcal{M}_{C}^{\mathrm{3d}}
is a hyper-Kähler manifold parameterized by the expectation values of gauge-invariant combinations of scalars in vector multiplets and monopole operators.

In an abelian gauge theory, the classical Coulomb branch is
\mathbb{R}^{3}\times S^{1}
where
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is spanned by the expectation values of the complex scalar
\varphi
and
\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}
is by that of the monopole operators
v^{\pm}=e^{\pm\frac{1}{g^{2}}(\sigma+i\gamma)}
. Note that
\gamma
is a periodic scalar
\gamma\sim\gamma+2\pi g^{2}
called “dual photon” subject to
d\gamma=\ast dA
.
However, it is well-known that a 3d
\mathcal{N}=4
Coulomb branch receives quantum corrections, which deform the classical moduli space. For instance, quantum corrected Coulomb branches in abelian gauge theories have been investigated in
(
BDG 17
, §3)
.

In a non-abelian gauge
G
theory, the scalars in the
\mathcal{N}=4
vector multiplet take expectation values in the Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{t}\subset\mathfrak{g}
. For generic expectation values, the gauge group is broken to a Cartan subgroup
T\cong\U(1)^{\rk(G)}\subset G
. Therefore, around a generic point, the Coulomb branch is locally
(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times S^{1})^{\rk(G)}
. However, the Weyl group
W
acts on the scalars as a residual gauge symmetry. In addition, it receives both perturbative and non-perturbative quantum corrections. In the end, the Coulomb branch is birationally equivalent
BFN 18
to

\mathcal{M}_{C}^{\mathrm{3d}}\approx\frac{(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times S^{1})^{\rk(G)}}{W}~.

(299)

Moreover, it admits an interpretation as an integrable system

\pi:\mathcal{M}_{C}^{\mathrm{3d}}\to\mathbb{C}^{\rk(G)}

(300)

where a generic fiber is
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{\rk(G)}
. This projection can be obtained by forgetting about monopole operators, and the base
\mathbb{C}^{\rk(G)}
is parametrized by the gauge-invariant operators
\Tr(\varphi^n)
of the complex scalar.

If a theory is the reduction of a 4d
\mathcal{N}=2
theory of class
\mathcal{S}
on
S^{1}
, the projection (
300
) can be interpreted as a partial decompactification of the corresponding Coulomb branch
\mathcal{M}_{C}(C,G,\mathsf{L})
. As explained in §
4.1
, the Coulomb branch
\mathcal{M}_{C}(C,G,\mathsf{L})
of a 4d
\mathcal{N}=2
theory
\mathcal{T}[C,G,\mathsf{L}]
of class
\mathcal{S}
on
S^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
where
S^{1}
is a circle of radius
R
is a hyper-Kähler manifold (say,
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{M}}_{H}(C,G)=4r
). It is moreover a completely integrable system so that a generic Hitchin fiber is a complex tori of volume
1/R^{r}
which is homeomorphic to
T^{2r}
. As
S^{1}
shrinks
R\to 0
, a generic fiber is decompactified to
(\mathbb{R}\times S^{1})^{r}
, and its volume diverges to infinity. Hence, a generic fiber and the base in (
300
) locally parametrized by the monopole operators and the complex scalars
\varphi
, respectively, are holomorphic in complex structure
I
.

D.2
3d
\mathcal{N}=4
Coulomb branches and Cherednik algebras

Trigonometric Cherednik algebra

Let us consider the 3d
\mathcal{N}=8
theory, which consists of a vector multiplet and a hypermultiplet in the adjoint representation. It is a reduction of the 4d
\mathcal{N}=4
theory on a circle
S^{1}
. The Coulomb branch of the 3d
\mathcal{N}=8
theory with gauge group
G
is actually

\mathcal{M}_{C}^{\mathrm{3d}}\Big[\!\raisebox{-9.95863pt}{\hbox to26.75pt{\vbox to21.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 18.51675pt\lower-10.77515pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 11.12 0 C 11.12 6.14 6.14 11.12 0 11.12 C -6.14 11.12 -11.12 6.14 -11.12 0 C -11.12 -6.14 -6.14 -11.12 0 -11.12 C 6.14 -11.12 11.12 -6.14 11.12 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{{}}{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{{}}{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{{}}{}}{{{}}{{}}}{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{{}}{}}{{{}}{{}}}{{}}{}{{}}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -9.87 -5.7 C -25.34 -14.63 -25.34 14.63 -9.87 5.7}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}~\Big]=\frac{\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{C}}\times T_{\mathbb{C}}^{\vee}}{W}~,

(301)

and it is indeed free from quantum corrections. Although the
\SU(2)_{C}
symmetry treats all the hyper-Kähler complex structures
(I,J,K)
equally, it is natural to take a viewpoint holomorphic in complex structure
J
to see the connection to Cherednik algebra. A supersymmetric Wilson loop
\Tr\,\exp\oint_{S^{1}}(A+\varphi)
in 4d reduces to the complex scalar
\varphi
in 3d whereas an ’t Hooft operator in 4d becomes a monopole operator in 3d. As a result,
\mathcal{M}_{C}^{\mathrm{3d}}
is the trigonometric degeneration of (
201
) by taking the Cartan subgroup
T_{\mathbb{C}}
to the Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{C}}
.
18
18

18

In
(
Che 05
, §1.6)
, the trigonometric limit is taken in the
S
-dual side,
T_{\mathbb{C}}^{\vee}\to\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\vee}
, instead. However, the trigonometric limit
T_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{C}}
of the Wilson loop is more natural from the physical viewpoint. Therefore, among the two polynomial representations in Appendix
B.1.7
, the difference-rational polynomial representation is more natural than the differential polynomial representation for the reduction from 4d to 3d.

It is clear from (
233
) that the quantized Coulomb branch of the 3d
\mathcal{N}=8
theory and its mass deformation are related to the spherical subalgebra of the trigonometric Cherednik algebra
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{tri}}(W)
. To see this, let us consider the case of rank one. If the gauge group is either
\SU(2)
or
\SO(3)
, the Coulomb branch (
301
) is
(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times})/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, which has two
A_{1}
singularities. However, the dual maximal torus is
T_{\mathbb{C}}^{\vee}=\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{C}}/\mathsf{Q}
where
\mathsf{Q}(\SU(2))=\mathsf{P}(\SO(3))\subset\mathsf{P}(\SU(2))=\mathsf{Q}(\SO(3))
so that the
\SO(3)
Coulomb branch is a double cover of the
\SU(2)
Coulomb branch. Therefore, the quantized Coulomb branch of the
\SO(3)
theory is isomorphic to the spherical trigonometric Cherednik algebra
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{tri}}_{\hbar,c=0}
at
c=0
. On the other hand, the quantized Coulomb branch of the
\SU(2)
theory is the
\xi_{2}
-invariant subalgebra of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{tri}}_{\hbar,c=0}
generated by

v=\mathbf{e}\hat{x}^{2}\mathbf{e}~,\quad\textrm{and}\quad y^{2}-1=(Y^{2}+1+Y^{-2})\mathbf{e}~,

where the notation is the same as Appendix
B.2.3
. In other words, the
\SO(3)
theory is endowed with the minimal monopole operator whereas the
\SU(2)
theory is not. This is consistent with what we have seen for ’t Hooft operators in the
\SO(3)_{+}
and
\SU(2)
4d
\mathcal{N}=4
theory, respectively, in §
4.1
.

Turning on mass parameters breaks a half of supersymmetries and it is called 3d
\mathcal{N}=4^{*}
theory. Correspondingly, the two
A_{1}
singularities turn into two exceptional divisors in the
\SO(3)
Coulomb branch. (Figure
22
.) The three complex structures
(I,J,K)
of the 3d
\mathcal{N}=4^{*}

\SO(3)
Coulomb branch
\mathcal{M}_{C}^{\mathrm{3d}}
inherits from those of the Coulomb branch
\mathcal{M}_{C}(C_{p},\SO(3)_{+})
in §
4.1
. At the classical level, the period integrals of
(\omega_{I},\omega_{J},\omega_{K})
over an exceptional divisor provide the parameters
(\talpha_{p},\tbeta_{p},{}_{p})
as in (
28
). Indeed, the complex mass parameter of the adjoint hypermultiplet is
\tbeta_{p}+i{}_{p}
whereas
\talpha_{p}
corresponds to the real mass parameter. The triple
(\talpha_{p},\tbeta_{p},{}_{p})
is transformed as the scalars
\Phi=(\sigma,\varphi)
in the
\mathcal{N}=4
vector multiplet under
\SU(2)_{C}
. Consequently, the quantized Coulomb branch of the
\SO(3)
3d
\mathcal{N}=4^{*}
theory is isomorphic to the spherical trigonometric Cherednik algebra
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{tri}}_{\hbar,c}
.

Therefore, the representation theory of the algebra can be studied in the context of brane quantization analogous to §
2
. However,
we should note that there is one difference between 3d
\mathcal{N}=4
theories and 4d
\mathcal{N}=2
theories. In trigonometric Cherednik algebra (
245
), one can set
\hbar=1
by redefinition
\hat{x}\to\hbar\hat{x}
,
c\to\hbar c
. Hence, we can consider the 2d
A
-model on the 3d
\mathcal{N}=4^{*}

\SO(3)
Coulomb branch
\mathfrak{X}
with a fixed symplectic form
\omega_{\mathfrak{X}}=-\omega_{K}
where the parameter of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{tri}}_{1,c}
is identified by
c=-\talpha_{p}/2
.
More generally, once we fix a complex structure of any 3d
\mathcal{N}=4
Coulomb branch in which deformation quantization is performed, a symplectic form can be set to a particular Kähler form thanks to the
\SU(2)_{C}
symmetry. On the other hand, a 4d
\mathcal{N}=2
superconformal theory is endowed with the
\U(1)_{r}\times\SU(2)_{H}

R
-symmetry, and
\U(1)_{r}
rotates only
\omega_{J}
and
\omega_{K}
. As a result,
\hbar
cannot be fixed to a particular value, and the story becomes more delicate as seen in §
2
.

Without loss of generality, we can hence consider that the target
\mathfrak{X}
of the 2d
A
-model is the 3d
\mathcal{N}=4^{*}

\SO(3)
Coulomb branch with

\talpha_{p}\neq 0~,\quad\tbeta_{p}=0={}_{p}

(302)

and the symplectic form is
\omega_{\mathfrak{X}}=-\omega_{K}
. The target can be understood as a partial decompactification of the Coulomb branch
\mathcal{M}_{C}(C_{p},\SO(3)_{+})
in §
4.1
with the same ramification parameters (
302
) along the
x
-direction. Upon 3d reduction, the singular fiber of type
I_{0}^{*}
in
\mathcal{M}_{C}(C_{p},\SO(3)_{+})\to\mathcal{B}_{u}
is decompactified to the left of Figure
22
, which consists of a cigar and the two exceptional divisors, denoted by
\mathbf{D}_{1}
and
\mathbf{D}_{3}
.
Consequently, finite-dimensional representations come from branes
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathbf{D}_{1}}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathbf{D}_{3}}
supported on the two exceptional divisors in Figure
22
that can exist only when
c=-(2\ell-1)/2
as in §
2.6.4
. Moreover, they are related by the
\xi_{2}
-reflection
\mathscr{D}_{\ell}^{(1)}\to\mathscr{D}_{\ell}^{(3)}
.
Indeed, the finite-dimensional representations of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{1,c}^{\textrm{tri}}
(and
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{1,c}^{\textrm{rat}}
) are classified by
BEG 03
;
Eti 07
;
VV 09
, and this conclusion is consistent with
(
BEG 03
, Proposition 7.1)
.

Let us remark the indications from the brane quantization on distinguished infinite-dimensional representations of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{tri}}_{1,c}
that stem from branes supported on a generic fiber
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
of (
300
) and the cigar in
\pi^{-1}(0)
. A brane supported on a generic fiber is labeled by a holonomy
y_{0}
of the
\Spin^{c}
-bundle around
S^{1}\subset\mathbb{C}^{\times}
and a position
v
of the base in (
300
). Hence, we denote the corresponding infinite-dimensional representation by
V_{\mathbb{C}^{\times}}^{y_{0},v}
. The
y
-weights of this representation are unbounded below and above. On the other hand, for a brane supported on the cigar, the
y
-weights of the corresponding representation, say
V_{\textrm{cigar}}
, are bounded below. The distinguished infinite-dimensional representations
V_{\mathbb{C}^{\times}}
and
V_{\textrm{cigar}}
are analogous to the principal and discrete series of
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
, respectively.

As in §
2.7
, we can consider bound states of branes. For instance, when
c=-(2\ell-1)/2
, the bound state of
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathbf{D}_{i}}
and the brane supported on the cigar gives rise to the short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\iota(V_{\textrm{cigar}})\longrightarrow V^{(i)}\longrightarrow\mathscr{D}_{\ell}^{(i)}\longrightarrow 0~,\qquad i=1,3~.

(303)

This is analogous to (
146
).
On the other hand, in the case of no holonomy with Ramond spin structure
y_{0}=+
, it can enter the singular fiber
\pi^{-1}(0)
when
c\in\mathbb{Z}
, which yields

0\longrightarrow\iota(V_{\textrm{cigar}})\longrightarrow V_{\mathbb{C}^{\times}}^{+,0}\longrightarrow V_{\pi^{-1}(0)}\longrightarrow 0~.

(304)

This is analogous to (
153
).

If
G=\SU(2)
, the mass deformation develops only one exceptional divisor
\mathbf{D}_{1}
(no
\mathbf{D}_{3}
) and the singular fiber
\pi^{-1}(0)
has one
A_{1}
singularity.
The quantized Coulomb branch of the 3d
\mathcal{N}=4^{*}

\SU(2)
theory is the
\xi_{2}
-invariant subalgebra of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{tri}}_{1,c}
.

We have seen that the Coulomb branch of the 4d
\mathcal{N}=4
theory on
S^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
can be constructed from affine Grassmannian Steinberg variety
\mathcal{R}
in (
199
).
Indeed, the Coulomb branch of the 3d
\mathcal{N}=8
theory can be obtained by taking the spectrum of the
G_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathscr{O}}
-equivariant Borel-Moore homology of
\mathcal{R}

BFM 05
;
BFN 18

(305)

By introducing the same equivariant action as in (
202
), we obtain the quantized Coulomb branch
H^{(G_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathscr{O}}\times\mathbb{C}_{t}^{\times})\rtimes\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{\hbar}}_{*}(\mathcal{R})
of the 3d
\mathcal{N}=4^{*}
theory.
If

, then it is isomorphic to the spherical subalgebra
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}_{\hbar,c}^{\textrm{tri}}(\mathfrak{S}_{N})
of the trigonometric Cherednik algebra of type

.

Motivated by this construction, the mathematical definition of Coulomb branches of general 3d
\mathcal{N}=4
quiver theories has been given in
BFN 18
;
BFN 19
;
BFN 17
.
In addition, representation theory of a 3d
\mathcal{N}=4
quantized Coulomb branch has been studied in the context of boundary conditions of 3d
\mathcal{N}=4
gauge theory in physics literature
BDGH 16
;
BDG+ 18
;
DFPY 19
. Their
K
-theoretic version will potentially lead to a vast generalization of DAHA as in
BEF 16
and its representation theory.

Rational Cherednik algebra

The Coulomb (and Higgs) branch of the 3d
\mathcal{N}=4
SQED with massless 2 flavors is
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Once the mass parameters are turned on, the
A_{1}
singularity of
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is resolved, and the resulting manifold is called the Eguchi-Hanson space
T^{*}\mathbb{C}\mathbf{P}^{1}
. From its complete hyper-Kähler metric, it can be regarded as
S^{1}
fibration over
\mathbb{R}^{3}
where the fiber shrinks at two points in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. For instance, the detail can be found in
(
GW 09
, §3.7)
. The deformation quantization of the Eguchi-Hanson space leads to the spherical subalgebra
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{rat}}_{\hbar,c}
of the rational Cherednik algebra that is isomorphic to the universal enveloping algebra of the
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
Lie algebra (
249
). The representation theory of this algebra has been exclusively investigated from the viewpoint of brane quantization in
GW 09
.
19
19

19

Precisely speaking, we need the
1/2
shift in
c
as in §
2.3.1
to connect to
GW 09
.
In particular, a brane supported on
\mathbb{C}\mathbf{P}^{1}\subset T^{*}\mathbb{C}\mathbf{P}^{1}
can exist only when
c=-(2\ell-1)/2
like
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathbf{D}_{i}}
in §
2.6.4
, and it gives rise to a finite-dimensional representation. On the other hand, branes supported on a cigar in Figure
22
or
T^{*}S^{1}\subset T^{*}\mathbb{C}\mathbf{P}^{1}
bring about a discrete or principal series of
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
. We refer the reader to
GW 09
for more details.

Now let us consider the 3d
\mathcal{N}=4

\U(N)
gauge theory with one adjoint and one fundamental hypermultiplet. If the theory is massless, the Coulomb branch
dBHO+ 97
is the
N
-th symmetric product of
\mathbb{C}^{2}

Consequently, its coordinate ring is indeed isomorphic to the spherical subalgebra of the
\mathfrak{gl}(N)
rational Cherednik algebra
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{rat}}(\mathfrak{gl}(N))
at
c=0=\hbar
. In this sense, the spherical rational Cherednik algebra
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{rat}}(\mathfrak{gl}(N))
can be interpreted as a “Lie algebra” of the spherical DAHA
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}(\mathfrak{gl}(N))
. Once mass parameters are turned on, the Coulomb branch is the Hilbert scheme of
N
-points on the affine plane
\mathbb{C}^{2}
, and the resolution of singularities can be understood as the Hilbert-Chow map

\pi:\mathrm{Hilb}^{N}\mathbb{C}^{2}\to\mathrm{Sym}^{N}\mathbb{C}^{2}~.

(306)

If we remove the center-of-mass coordinate of
\mathrm{Hilb}^{N}
, we have the spherical subalgebra
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{rat}}(\mathfrak{S}_{N})
of the rational Cherednik algebra of type
A_{N-1}
.

In the case of type
A_{N-1}
, it is known that
\textrm{Spec}(S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{rat}}_{\hbar=0}(\mathfrak{S}_{N}))
has a unique compact holomorphic Lagrangian submanifold, called the punctual Hilbert scheme, which is the preimage
\pi^{-1}(0)
of zero under the Hilbert-Chow map (
306
).
As briefly discussed in §
3.1.2
, this will give rise to the unique finite-dimensional representation of
S\!\accentset{\mbox{\large.\hskip-1.33333pt.}}{H}^{\textrm{rat}}(\mathfrak{S}_{N})
when
c=-M/N
with coprime
(M,N)
. This is consistent with the classification of finite-dimensional representations of the spherical rational Cherednik algebra
(
BEG 03
, Theorem 1.2 and 1.10)
. (See also
GS 05
;
GS 06
for a realization by geometric quantization.) Moreover, the finite-dimensional module is isomorphic to the lowest
a
-degree of the HOMFLY-PT homology of the
(M,N)
torus knot
GORS 14
.

\mathcal{N}=4

\U(N)
gauge theory with
\ell
hypermultiplets in the fundamental representation
dBHO+ 97
is the
N
-th symmetric power of the hypersurface
\mathcal{S}_{\ell}
determined by the equation

in
\mathbb{C}^{3}
, which can be expressed as

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}~\Big]=\textrm{Sym}^{N}(\mathcal{S}_{\ell})~.

The deformation quantization of the coordinate ring of the Coulomb branch leads to the
cyclotomic rational Cherednik algebras

KN 18
;
OY 19
. The
\ell=0
and
\ell=1
specializations are the spherical subalgebras of the trigonometric and rational Cherednik algebras of type
\mathrm{GL}(N,\mathbb{C})
, respectively. Hence, their representation theory can be similarly investigated in the context of brane quantization, which deserves future study.
